The United States Political Adviser has the honor to
nclose herewith copies of the English translation of
Revised Agricultural Land Adjustment Law" and "Bill for
2/
the Special Measure Br the Establishment of Owner-Farmer."
Both of these bills were passed by the Diet during its
last session.
894.52/11
In order to implement the "Bill for the Special
Measure for the Establishment of Owner Farmer," it was
necessary to effect considerable revision of the old
Agricultural Land Adjustment Law. The underscored por-
tions of Enclosure No. 1 are the sections which were
revised or deleted.
Under the provisions of the new owner-farmer legisla-
-1246
tion, the large estates will be broken up. The Government
will purchase all lands declared by the various Agricultural
COPILS
Land Commissions to be a vailable for distribution. After
PC:
a property has been acquired it will immediately be made
available for sale to an owner-farmer. The acreage which
can be purchased varies acccrding to location, the largest
acreage per person being in Hokkaido. Purchasers will be
permitted to pay for property acquired over a period of
thirty years with interest at the rate of 3.2 per cent
being charged on the principal.

Explanatbry Notes:
The upper section represents the proposed revised legislation with under-
lining to indicate the portion that differs from the present Law.
The lower section represents existing legislation corresponding to the pro-
posed legislation. Where no reforms are proposed in existing legislation, the
legislation is included in the upper section, and is omitted from the lower.
(TN: In place of "upper and lower sections", indentation will be used to differ-
entiate between proposed and existing legislation. Proposed reform legislation
(i.e. the upper section of the original document) will run from the margin, while
existing legislation (i.e. the lower section of the document) will be indented
one inch. The portion which ig side-lined in the document will be underlined
been made.
Article I.
This law has fr its object tho adjustment of mattors pertaining to agricul-
tural lands in crder to stahilize the status of farmers and to maintain and
promote agricultural produe'ive capacity.'
Article I.
This law has fo its objoct the adjustiment of matters pertaining.
to agricultural Lands in order to regenerate the cconomy of agri-
cultural villagcs and maintain peace therein, bearing in mind the
necd for stabilizing the status of farmers and farm-owners and
maintaining and promoting, in a spirit of mutual assistance, agri-
cultural productive capacity.
Articlc II.
In this legislation, the torm "agricultural land" shall mean land which can be
used for farming.
The term "farm ront" is applied to the rent for leased land vhich is used for
farming. ( Under the torm "rent" the following things are included: land which
is outside of the farm proper; and buildings and other installations which are
leased with the farm. In cases where this rent is inseparable by contract from
the farm rent, the rent from the land outside of the farm and from buildings and
installations on the farm vill bo included in the farm rent.) Th "farm ront"
shall apply also to cases of emphytousis when the object is the cultivation of
land.
Article III.
Whenever the farmer or faim ormer is unable to farm or manage the farm because
of illness, or is oxcused under exicting regulations, he may apply for manage-
ment or sale of the farm to the city, towm or village; to the agricultural
association of the city, town or village; or to other such organizations that
have been constituted by order.
When such an application is made, the aforementioned organizations have the
authority to manage or purchase the farm in accordance with the order.
Articlc IV.
The creation or transfor of the ownorship, luase, superficies, or other farm
land rights cannot be made unloss the party obtains the permission of the pro-
vincial governor or the approval of thc Agricultural Land Commission of the
city, town, or village concerned in accordance with the provisions of the order.'
The permission. or approval mentioned in the preceding paragraph may be subject
to condition. Action takon without the permission or approval montioned in the
first paragraph shall be invalid.

If the purchaser does not make a deposit or complete payment on the
evaluation of the property to the owner of the land or install-
ations in accordance with the provisions of Article IV, Section 5,
Paragraph 2 on or before the time set by the adjudication, the de-
cision is no longer valid.
Article IV. Section 8.
A party dissatisfied with the decision made under the provisions of
Article IV, section 3, paragraph 2 or 3 may appeal the decision to
the competent Ministry. If a person feels his rights have been in-
fringed upon through an illegal decision, he may instituto a law-
suit in the administrative courts.
Litigation which can be filed in ordinary courts in accordance with
the provisions of paragraph 4 cannot be filed as an administrative
suit.
Persons who are dissatisfied with the evaluation adjudication ren-
dered under the provisions of Article IV, section 3, paragraph 2 or
3 may file suit in ordinary courts. However, a person cannot filo
suit if more than three months have elapsed since he received a cer-
tificd written copy of the adjudication.
When the purchaser files suit in accordance with the provisions of
the preceding paragraph, he may make a deposit cquivalent to the pre-
determined evaluation. However, if the seller demands, the purcha-
sor must pay the deposit set by the seller.

Dear General:
18
As you are aware, the Japansse Government, under consiésrable
prodding from the Supreme Commander, has started an extensive pre-
gram of referm in land tenure and conditions affesting the farming
population. As this program is important to the achievement of ear
objectives in Japan, it appears most desirable that ScaP Meadquarters
give it all possible supervision and assistance.
The Department of State believes that a small but strong missiom
of agricultural specialists would bs able te lend scap sabstastial
support during the early stages of the reform program. I mnderstand
2
that this matter was raised with SoaP a fow months ago and that ke
approved the idea of an agricultural mission previded its membars
2746
remain not less than aix months (Cu-I 1744, 11 Jaly, 1946). Ia a
teletype conference between Washingtan and Tokyo on October 10, the
Natural Resources Section of SCar Headquarters expressed interest
in obtaining personnel to supervise the agrariam referm plans. the
six months' requiremsnt seens to us reasonable in view ef the com-
plexity of the situation.
It is the opinion of this Department that the proposed mission
should include:
(a) Twe specialists in general agricultural econemics to deal
with problems of temare and tenaney!
(b) One specialist in agricultural finamce to assist in the ro-
organization of credit institutions and to advise on tax problems;
(c) Ome specialist in agricultural organisational problems to
aid in the expansion and democratisation of rural co-eperatives and
other farm groups.
Since one of the principal purposes of the mission would be to
encourage and assist the Japansse in introdacing thoss institutions
Hajor General Daniel Noce,
Chief, Civil Affaire Davision,

Imperial Ordinance Number 621:
Order to Carvig Ont the
ASpecial Measure for the Establishment of Owner-Farmers.
ARTICLE ONE. The exceptions to Article Two, Paragraph Three
of the Special Measure for the Establishment of Owner-Farmers
shall be the following:
A. Illness
B. School attendance
C. Those drafted for military service prior to
15 August 1945.
D. Those persons who were elected to public offiee who

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.99)
are regarded indispensable for the moment by Shi-Cho-Som
Agricultural Land Board and whose decision is approved by the
To-Do-Fu-Ken Agricultural Land Board.
ARTICLE TwQ. The Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Land Board, when
carrying cut the specifications designated in Article Three,
Paragraph One, Item One of the Special Measure for the Estab-
lishment of Owner-Farmers, shall secure the consent of the
Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Land Board established in the neighbor-
ing Shi-Cho-Son, and shall express approval to the To-Do-Fu-Kan
Agricultural Land Board before the date designated by the loeal
governor.
ARTICLE THREE. The farmlands designated in Article Three,
Paragraph Four of the Special Measure for the Establishment
of Owner-Farmers shall be as follows:
A. Farmland whose yield is highly unpredictable, such
as newly reclaimed land, scorched fields, and transformed plots
of land.
B. Land whose proximity to metal mines and coal minos
endangers its usage for farming.
C. In addition to the two types of land mentioned above,
those lands designated by the Agriculture and Forestry Minister.
ARTICLE FOUR. The authorization provided for in Artiele Three,
Paragraph Five of the Speeial Measure for the Establishment of
Owner-Farmers shall be exercised by Shi-Cho-Son Agrieultural
Land Board of the To-Do-Fu-Ken Agricultural Land Board in
matters concerning land mentioned in Item One to Five in the
same Paragraph: and by the Shi-Cho-Son Agrieultural Land
Board in matters concernin land mentioned in Item Six of the
same paragraph.
ARTICLE FIVE. with regards to Items Cne or Two of Artiele
Three, Paragraph Five of the Special Measure for the Establish-
ment of Owner-Farmers, when either of the following 1tems apply
to an independent farmer or to a corporation, their contimmed
cultivation activity is justifiable.
A. With regard to independent cultivation, when the
operator exerts his utmost individual effort to attain the
most efficient cultivation of the land or when a deerease in
productivity is considered inevitable in cases where the land
concerned is subdivided.
B. With regard to corporations and other bodies, when a
2

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
decrease in productivity is considered inevitable in eases
where the land coneerned is subdivided or when the eultive-
tion of the land is considered indispensable for the com-
tinued operation of its main occupation.
ARTICLE SIX. Those groups designated in Article Five, Item
Two of the Special Weasure for the Establishment of Owner-
Farmers shali be bodies organized by Buraku, industrial
associations, silk-worm reeling associations, and other bodies
organized by cultivators and shall be designated by the re-
gional chief.
ARTICLE SEVEN. Matters decided by Article Five, Item Six of
the Special Measure for the Establishment of Independent
Farmers shall be as follows:
A. chool attendance.
Those drafted for military service prior to 15 August
1945.
C. Those persons who were elected to publie office and
who are regarded indispensable for the moment by Shi-Cho-Son
Agricultural Land Board and whose decision is approved by
To-Do-Fu-Ken Agricultural Land Board.
ARTICLL EIGHT. The farmland designated in Artiele Five, Item
Seven of the Special Measure for the Establishment of Owner-
Farmers shall be as follows:
A. Farmland which can be subdivided.
B. Land whose proximity to retal mines and coal mines
endangers its usage for farming.
C. In addition to the two types of land designated above,
those lands designated by the Agriculture and Forestry Minister.
ARTICLE NINE. The Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Land Board or the
To-Do-Fu-Ken Agricultural Board shall determine the price of
farmland which has not been evaluated in accordance with the
Land Tax Law by relying on Article Six, Iten Three of the
Special Measure for the Establishment of Owner-Farmers (Article
Twenty Eight, Paragraph Two; Article Thirty One, Paragraph
Three; Article Thirty Six, Paragraph Three; Article Thirty
Seven, Paragraph Two; Article Thirty Eight, Paragraph Two and
Article Forty One, Paragraph Three shall also be included) or
when evalueting farmland in accordance with the same paragraph,
the price set on it shall not exceed the current value of a
3

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.99)
like land in the vicinity.
ARTICLE TEH. When the regional chief approves the valuatiom
set by the Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Board in aceordanee with
Article Six, Paragraph Three of the Special Measure fer the
Establishment of Owner-Farmers (including Artiele Twenty-
Eight, Paragraph Two; Article Thirty-Eight, Paragraph Two;
and Article Forty-One, Paragraph Three of the same law) he
must first listen to the opinion of the To-Do-Fu-Ken Agri-
cultural Land Board.
ARTICLE RLEVEN. When the Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Committeo
places value on agricultural installations, residences and
constructions, which it intends to purchase in aceordanee
with Article Fifteen, Paragraph One of the Special Measure
for the Establishment of the Owner-Farmer it must rely on
the rules established by the Central Agricultural Board.
A valuation placed on pastureland in accordance with
Article Fifteen, Paragraph Three of the Special Measure for
the Esteblishment of Independent Farmers shall not exceed the.
amount determined by the Central Agricultural Committee which
evaluated the land to approximate the current value of a like
land in the neighborhood.
ARTICLL TWELVE. With regard to farmland owned by the govern-
ment, (with the exceptions of farmlands purchased under the
provisions in Article Three or Article Thirty, Paragraph One,
Item Three of the Special Measure for the Establishment of
Independent Farmers), when the Shi-Cho-Son Agrieultural Land
Board decides that these lands should be offered for the
purpose of realizing the estahlishuent of Owner-Farmers, this
matter will be supervised by the Agriculture and Porestry
Minister.
The above-mentioned decision will not become effeetive
unless prior approval of the To-Do-Fu-Ken Agricultural Land
Board is secured.
In order that the To-Do-Fu-Ken Agricultural Land Board
can give approval to the above, it must secure the approval of
the minister who has juricietion over the land coneerned.
The minister coneerned can have either the head of a
section or bureau, or a repional head, exercise his authority
in accordance vith the above-rentioned provision.

ARTICLE ONE. The exceptions to Artiele Two, Paragraph Three
of the Special Measure for the Establishment of Owner-Farmers
shall be the following:
A. Illness
B. School attendance
C. Those drafted for military service prior to
15 August 1945.
D. Those persons who were eleeted to public offiee who

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.99)
are regarded indispensable for the moment by Shi-Cho-Son
Agricultural Land Board and whose decision is approved by the
To-Do-Fu-Ken Agrioultural Land Board.
ARTICLE TWO. The Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Land Board, when
carrying out the specifications designated in Article Three,
Paragraph One, Item One of the Special Measure for the Estab-
lishment of Owner-Farmers, shall secure the consent of the
Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Land Board established in the neighbor-
ing Shi-Cho-Son, and shall express approval to the To-Do-Fu-Ken
Agricultural Land Board before the date designated by the loeal
governor.
ARTICLE THREE. The farmlands designated in Artiele Three,
Paragraph Four of the Special Measure for the Establishment
of Owner-Farmers shall be as follows:
A. Farmland whose yield is highly unpredictable, such
as newly reelaimed land, scorched fields, and transformed plots
of land.
B. Land whose proximity to metal mines and coal mines
endangers its usage for farming.
C. In addition to the two types of land montioned above,
those lands designated by the Agriculture and Forestry Minister.
ARTICLE FOUR. The authorization provided for in Article Three,
Paragraph Five of the Special Measure for the Establishment of
Owner-Farmers shall be exercised by Shi-Cho-Son Agrieultural
Land Board of the To-Do-Fu-Ken Agricultural Land Board in
matters concerning land mentioned in Item One to Five in the
same Paragraph: and by the Shi-Cho-Son Agricultural Land
Board in matters concerning land mentioned in Item Six of the
same paragraph.
ARTICLE FIVE. With regards to Items One or Two of Artiele
Three, Paragraph Five of the Special Measure for the Establish-
ment of Owner-Farmers, when either of the following items apply
to an independent farmer or to a corporation, their contimmed
cultivation activity is justifiable.
A. With regard to independent cultivation, when the
operator exerts his utmost individual effort to attain the
most efficient cultivation of the land or when a decrease in
productivity is considered inevitable in cases where the land
concerned is subdivided.
B. With regard to corporations and other bodies, when a

きを基準とすれば、政府は買收できないが二十年十一月二十三日現在を
第四十五條は報告を徴する権開が規定されてみる。
基準とすればそれは小作地となつて買收できることにもなる。又現行農
第四十六條は政府が寶渡し又は使用する土地その他の管理に關する規
地調整法を脱法せんとして虚偽の申請により地方長官の認可を受けて小
定である。農地については先に逃べた通り買收の期日と賢渡の期日とを
作地を小作人以外に譲渡した場合もこの親定を適用して以前の地主の所
できるだけ一致させて管理の手数を省くつもりであるが、時としては兩
有する小作地とすることができる。尤も地方長官が正當に認可して農地
者の間に距離があり又小作農が買受けぬ場合もあらう。それで管理規則
の護渡があつだもの、選地調整法第九條第一項の土地取上の條件にもあ
をつくつて小作農の選擇なり小作料の取立なりを市町村長、市町村濃地
てはまり小作人も承知し市町村農地委員會も承認した土地返還には事
委員會、市町村農菜會等に委任する考である。
質上この規定を及す必要はあるまい。市町村農地委員會がこの規定を適
第四十七條は市町村農地委員會の活動が不活設で買收や変渡が豫期の
用した場合は事態を明かにして都道府縣農地委員會の審査を受ける必要
様に進まない場合は都道村縣護地委員會に命してその事務を處理させ都
があるので買收計鑑にその理由を明記せしめる様にするつもりである
道府縣農地委員會の活動も不充分な場合は地方長官又は中央農地委員會
に處理させ、事薬の進歩を岡るための規定である。
第四十八條は農地調整法によつて一市町村に二以上の地區農地委員會
ができた場合の規定である。在村地主か不在地主かは地區農地委員會の
區域で決定される。第四十九條は町村組合六大都市等に於ける特例第
五十條及び第五十一條は罰則である。
次に附則であるが頂要な規定が入つてある。農地の買收は買收計畫を
たてる際の事實に基いて小作地であるか、自作地であるか、在村地主であ
るか不在地主であるかをきめるのが原則であるが、第一次農地改革に前
後して小作地の不當取上や農地の不當資買等が行はれて農村に波殺を起
して居り、これらを獣過することは農地改革の趣旨にも添はないので
市町村農地委員會が相當と認めたときは昭和二十年十一月二十三日現在
の事質に基いて農地買收計盤を定めることができることこした。同日以
後地主が小作地を不當に取上げて自作してみる場合買牧計確をたてると
九1

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.94)
九〇
場合などは當然除外される豫定である。
て櫛利を有してみた者の生活を保護するために更に他の土地を買收し又
第二十九條は第十五條の規定によつて政府が買收した盤業用施設土地
は使用し得る規定第三十八條は前述の市町村農地委員會が未墾地買收
又は建物の費渡に闘する規定で農地の場合と同様に考へてよい。
計盤をたてる場合の規定である。この主務大臣の定める面積と云ふの
六

た方が小作が耕作するよりも生産力があがり、且つ土靴の取上によって
トンテ」極利を取得する場合は認可を不要としたへめ、地まが土地を護
小作人が生活に困らないと云ふ條件があつてこへに云地主の自作を相
渡する場合の脱法手段に用ひられるばかりでなく耕作を目的とする貸借
當とする場合その他土地の取上に正當な理由がある場合に限るとした。
機の設定又は護渡が全く統制の埒外に置かれ、農地の有效利用の面から
第二は土地の取上はこの様な條件がある上に市町村農地委員會の承認を
極めと不合理な結果となるので改正法ではこの例外を例除して、この第
必要とし承認なくして土地の取上を行へば罰則がかへることになつてみ
六條を第五條にした。

第六條は農地の潰般の制限に関する新しい規定である。潰地の制限は
村農地委員會の委員の選要が三月に施行飛定のところ今に至るまで施行
戦時中臨時農地等管理令によつて行はれたのであるが、戰後工場の敷地
することを得なかつたので土地取上の事例は相當多く各地に紛機を起し
として潰地が大皿に生することは豫想されないので現行法ではこれを削
た。今秋からは態々小作料の金納化が現質の間題となるし、小作地の開
除した。ところが、最近熟田熟畑を宅地に使用する傾向が段々増加して
放は態々本格的となるので土地取上は再び激化する情勢にあるが。これ
來たばかりでなく、自作農創設特別指置法による農地の買收を妨げるた
を放任しては選地改革の変味は大半失はれる危險もあるので、土地の取
めに故意に農地を潰すと云ふ傾向の生ずることも愛へられるので、農地
上は更にこれを制限することへした。その方法の第一は土地の取上と云
の潰般は地方長官の許可を要することした。尤も自作選創設特別指置
つても現行法では「貸貸借ノ解約又へ契約更新ノ拒絶」であつて「貸代
法案にある都市計畫關係の農地、使用目的變更豫定の農地、新開熱地
借ノ解除」は抑へてみない「買貸借解除」は借務下履行による債機

は地方長官の許可を不要とするつもりである。この許可には第二項で條
のが從來の法理論であつた。ところが最近の傾向として相當以前に行は
件を附けることができる。例へば工場敷地とすることは治支へないが煙

害の防除施設の工事を條件として許可する如きである。

In reply refer to
JK
My dear Mr. Yasui:
Thank you for your letter of February 14, 1947 concerning the
condemnation by the Japanese Government of agricultural land held
by Mr. Nakagoro Nishimoto.
The Japanese Diet has passed, and the Japanese Government is
894.52
now beginning to administer, an agricultural refomm law calling
for the purchase by the Government, for resale to tenants, of:
(a) all land held by absentee landlords, (b) all land held for
rental by non-absentee landlords in excess of one cho, and (c)
all land held by owner cultivators in excess of three cho.
52/2-1447
Presumably the land which Mr. Nishimoto owms is being
purchased by the Government under the terms of the law referred
to above.
You inquire as to whether this matter is under the jurisdic-
tion of the American Military Government in Japan. The answer is
that the law was brought into being under the general terms
of a directive from the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
dv
to the Japanese Government, and that its enforcement is being
supprvised by the occupation forces.
You also inquire as to what steps Mr. Nishimoto might take
if he does not wish to have his land purchased by the Government.
There would appear to be no such steps possible under the terms
of the law quoted above.
If I can be of any further assistance to you, please do not
hesitate to let me know.
CS/
Sincerely yours,
915
20

Division of Japanese and
Korean Economig Affairs

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.98)
SWEDISH VICE-CONSULATE
Department of Japanese Interests
Honolulu 3, T. H.
Apr1l 30, 1947
Legation of Sweden
Washington 8, D.C.
Attention of Mr. K. H. Andersson, Attache
Dear Sir:
We are as yet without authentic information regarding
the new Land Reform Lew of Japan. Rumors arising from private
letters to Japanese nationals residing here are that land held
by non-resident owners will be subject to sale by the Govern-
ment unless the cwner makes application for extension of time
for him to travel to Japan to attend to the disposal of the land
himself.
Requests by letter and cable to the Swedish Diplomatic
Representative in Tokyo have brought only the reply that he has
made repeated requests to the proper department of the Imperial
Government but received no response. In the meantime I am filling
out and certifying applications of land owners residing here,
for extension of time. These applications are addressed to the
Village Head of the village in which the land is located and
mailed to him with a letter asking him to acknowledge receipt
and advise what action may be taken on the petition. This is
"a shot in the dark", in the hope that it may result in giving
the absentee owner time to protect his interest as it may be
affected by the new law.
Would it be possible for your office to obtain any infor-
mation on this subject from the U. S. State Department? In thi
connection, what nation's governmont is now acting as the pro-
tecting power for Japan on the mainland United States? Fathers
(Japanese nationals) residiny here have sons on the mainland who
hold title to land in Japan. The fathers wish to advise them to
make application for extension of time to dispose of the land.
To whom should they go to have their signature to such applica-
tion certified? I regret that I am not able to answer them on
this point. They could have the application notarized, but a
consular official representing Japanese interests would have to
certify to the authenticity of the notary.
I shall appreciate any information and advice you can
give me in response to the above.
Very truly yours,
Gr W llong
GUSTAF W. OLSON
Vice-Consul

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokye, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
COPY
SWEDISH DIPLOMATIC LISSICN
Ref. BB-122/47.
URGENT
Note Verbale.
The Swedish Diplomatic Mission in Japan presents its
compliments to the Imperial Ministry for Foreign Affairs and,
referring to the former's note verbale BB-84/47, dated 6th
March, 1947, has the honour to indicate that, since the Japan-
ese nationals who reside in Hawaii and own land in Japan, are
greatly concerned about the protection of their interests, it
would be highly desirable that the Imperial Ministry, without
delay, provide this Mission with copies of the new Farm Land
Law as recuested in above-mentioned note verbale. Japanese
nationals in Hawaii have also raised the question whether the
disposition of their land under the new Reform Law can be post-
poned on application by those who wish to go to Japan in the
near future and this Mission has the honcur to request the
Imperial Ministry to furnish its comments thereon.
Tokyo, 25th April, 1947.
Imperial Ministry for Foreign Affairs,
Tokyo.

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
COPY
SWEDISH DIPLOMATIC LISSION
Ref. BB-173/47
Note Verbale
The Swedish Diplomatic Mission in Japan presents its
compliments to the Imperial Ministry for Foreign Affairs and
has the honour to acknowledge receipt of the latter's note
verbale No. 31/R.J., dated 30th April, 1947, with copies of
the new Farm Land Law which have been duly transmitted to the
Swedish Vice-Consulate at Honolulu, in charge of Japanese
interests in the Territory of Hawaii.
The said Vice-Consulate has, today, further communicated
to this Mission the following:
"The anxiety over the provisions of Japanese Farm Land
Law is very great among the nunerous owners living in
the Territory of Hawaii. The first notice of sale
seen here was received on 6th May by Suekichi Kikuchi
from the Chairman of the Farmland Committee at Fuku-
shima-ken, Adachi-gun, Motomiya-machi, postmarked 28th
March, and notifying him that his land would be sold on
3lst March. This seems unreasonably unfair to owners
here, most of whom are elderly people who planned to
return to their homeland to live on their land, and
who have ample means to do so as soon as permission
can be obtained from the United States State Depart-
ment. Please appeal to the Japanese Government
Authorities to grant an extensjon of time to owners
here, who make an application."
The Mission has the honour to request the Imperial
inistry to furnish its comments, if any, on the above, upon
receipt of which this Mission will transmit them to the Royal
Vice-Consulate at Honolulu.
Tokyo, 16th May, 1947.
COPY

=== Page 206 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 3 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
COPY
No. 35/LJ
Note Verbale.
The Japanese Linistry of Foreign Affairs presents its
compliments to the Swedish Diplomatic Mission in Japan, and
has the honor to acknovledge the receipt, on 5 May 1947, of
the latter's Note Verbale BB-122/47 dated 25 April 1947,
concerning the Japanese nationals residing in the Territory
of Hawaii and the new Farm Land Law of Japan.
In reply, the Ministry begs to state that the copies of
the new Farm Land Law of Japan, the request for which was
renewed in the Mission's Note Verbale under reference, have been
forwarded under cover of the Ministry's Note Verbale No. 3l/R.J.
of 30 April 1947 addressed to the Mission. The Ministry will
appreciate it if the Mission is good enough to take appropriate
action with respect to them.
As regards the question mentioned in the concluding portion
of the reference Note Verbale of the Mission, as to whether, in
the case of the Japanese nationals in the Hawaii who wish to come
to Japan in the near future, the disposition of their land under
the new Farm Land Law can be postponed on application, the views
of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry of Japan are to the
following effect:
1. Under the farm land reform program, which is scheduled
to be completed by the end of 1948, the buying up of farm land
is already in progress. To postpone the buying-up procedure in
the case of some particular non-resident owners would not be
permissible from the viewpoint of providing the prospective buyers
with fair and equal opportunities of buying and also from other
overall considerations. However, the land custodians in Japan
who represent the interests of non-resident owners in the latter's
behalf, may make such complaints as may be necessary to the
Agricultural Land Commission of the city, town or village concerned,
in respect of the land to be bought up, the terms and procedures
of buying up, etc. The non-resident owners themselves may, if
necessary, address without delay to the said Commission, in
writing, what they have to say in connection with these matters.
2. Under the farm land reform program, as is stipulated
in the relevant laws and regulations, the rule is that a farm
land, of which the owner, as of 23 November 1945, is not resident
in the same city, town or village where the farm land is located,
the said farm land is necessarily to be bought up as the farm
land of a non-resident owner, except in cases where the owner is
away from the said locality owing to admission to a school,
disease, military enlistment, or other unavoidable causes, and
where there is also such special cause as indicates that the said
owner has a close relationship with the said locality. However,
the stipulation relating to "cases where there is a special cause",
which

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 3 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
- 2 -
which enables a non-resident owner to be regarded as a resident
owner in this connection, is interpreted in a very narrow sense;
the fact of residing in a foreign country is not deemed to be
such a cause, and consequently does not warrant the application
of the special exceptional provisions.
Such being the case, the farm lands in Japan of Japanese
nationals in the Hawaii, even if they shortly return to this
country, are necessarily to be bought up as those of non-resident
owners.
3. In short, if the Japanese nationals in the Hawaii rely
upon the custodians of their farm lands in Japan in safeguarding
their interests, the custodians may be expected to take all the
necessary steps in their behalf. 1f, however, they find them-
selves unable to rest thus assured, they may directly address
without delay, in writing, statements of their cases to the
Agricultural Land Commission (Nochi-iinkai) of the city, town or
village concerned.
The Foreign Ministry would be grateful if the Mission would
take appropriate steps to cause the above to be brought to the
knowledge of the interested Japanese nationals residing in Hawaii.
In so far as the foregoing paragraph 3 is concerned, the ministry
will be glad to act, if necessary as through channel between the
absentee owners and Land Commissions.
27 May 1947.
Swedish Diplomatic Mission,
T o k y o.

A BRIEF EXPLANATICN ALOUT THE FAR. LAND
REFORL PROGRAILE FOR JAPANESE NATIONALS ABROAD.
Prepared by the ministry of Foreign Affairs.
1. Introduction:
On December 9tn 1945, the Japanese Government received a
memorandum from the General Headquarters, Supreme Comaander for
the Allied Powers, under the subject of "Rural Land Reform"
which reads, in part, as follows: (see the appendix)
"In order that the Imperial Japanese Government shall re-
move economic obstacles to the revival and strengthening of
democratic tendencies, establish respect for the dignity of man
and destroy the economic bondage which has enslaved the Japanese
farmer for centuries of feudal oppression, Imperial Government
is directed to take measures to insure that those who till the
soil of Japan shall have a more equal opportunity to enjoy the
fruits of their labor."
"The Japanese Iperial Government is therefore ordered to
submit to this Headquarters on or before 15 March 1946 a pro-
gram of rural land reform. This program shall contain plans
for:
a.
Transfer of land ownership from absentee land owners
to land operators.
b.
Provisions for purchase of farm lands from non-
operating owners at ecuitable rates.
C.
Provisions for tenant purchase of land at annual
instrunents commensurate with tenant income.
d.
Provisions for reasonable protection of former tenants
against reversion to tenancy status ....."
Upon receipt of the above memorandum preparations in compli-
ance with the memorandun were made by the Japanese Government
and the laws setting the programmes were enacted by the Diet
and were put into force on December 29th 1946.
It is stipulated for the Government to see to that
the entire Programme be completed by the end of 1948, and
farm lands of approximately two million (2,000,000) "Cho-bu"
(one "Cho-bu" corresponds to about two and hal acres) are
expected to be bought up and resold by the Government to the
tenant farmers engaged in cultivation at the time of purchase
or other persons likely to devote themselves to cultivation
as

=== Page 210 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 4 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Refora Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
-3-
as owner-farmers. It is reported as at the end of March 1947
that farm land of about one hundred and fifty thousand (150,000)
"Cho-bu" have already been purchased under the said Programme.
2. Legislations for the Programme.
The Farm Land Reform Programme consists of two major
legislations; Owner Farmer Establishment Special Measure Law
and Agricultural Land Adjustment Law, the former stipulating
the procedure by which the Government purchases the farm lands
and distributes them among tenant-farmers, the latter providing
for the establishment of Agricultural Land Commission, which is
the executive organ of this Programme, and is responsible for
the control of lease, transfer and price of farm land.
3. Lands to be purchased by the Government under the Programme.
(1) The following three kinds of land are mandatorily to
be purchased by the Government.
a. Non-resident owner's tenant-farmer land.
All the tenant-farmer land owned by a person who has his
permanent residence ("Jusho") outside the limit of a City,
Town or Village in which his tenant-farmer land is situated.
b. Portion of resident owner's tenant-farmer land exceeding
one (1) "Cho-bu" (in Hokkaido four (4) "Chobu").
c. Portion of resident owner's tenant-farmer land which
makes the total with the farm land cultivated by the owner
area to exceed three (3) "Cho-bu" (in Hokkaido twelve (12)
"Cho-bu".)
(2) The following six kinds of land is purchased by
the Government when the Agricultural Land Coumission concerned
deems it suitable for the purpose of establishing owner-farmers.
a. The owmer-farmer land exceeding three (3) "Cho-bu" and
the cultivation of which does not seen efficient.
b. The owner-farmer land cultivated by a person other
than the owner by contract other than tenancy.
c. The farm land owned and operated by juridical person
or other cooperation vhose cultivation efficiency is
deemed poor.
d. The tenant-farmer land owned by juridical person or
other coorporation.
e. The farm land left uncultivated by a person entitled
to cultivate it.
f. Other farm land offered by owners for sale.
4. Farm

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States rolitical Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
-4-
4. Farn Lands of Non-resident owmer abroad.
Under the Farm Land Reform Programme, as is stipulated in
the relevant laws and regulations, it is provided that a farm
land, of which the owner, retroactively as of 23 November 1945,
is not resident in the City, Town or Village where the farm
land is located, the same land is necessarily to be bought up
as farm land belonging to non-resident owner, except in cases
where the owmer is temporarily away from the said locality
for school attendance, medical care, military enlistment or
other unavoidable causes. However, the stipulation relating
to "unavoidable causes" which enables a non-resident owner to
be regarded as a resident owner in this connection, is
interpreted in a very narrow sense; the fact of residing in
a foreign country is not deemed to be such a cause, and con-
secuently does not warrant the application of the special
exceptional provisions.
1t is evident therefore, that the farm land in Japan
owned by Japanese nationals whether at home or abroad, even
if they shortly return to the spot, inevitably to be pur-
chased by the Government as farn land of non-resident owners.
5. Executive Organs for the Farm Land Reform Programme.
The plan of purchase, consideration (price) and time of
purchase are decided not by the Government itself but by
the Agricultural Land Commission of a City, Towm or Village,
which is usually composed of ten elected members, three from
land owners not engaged in cultivation, five from tenant
farmers and two from owner-farmers. When the Agricultural
Land Commission of a City, Town or Village sets up the plan
for the purchase of the farm land, public notice thereof
shall be served without delay and the document stating the
following matters be subaitted to public inspection at the city,
or village office for ten days from the day on which public
notice has been iven.
a. Name and permanent residence of the owner of the
agricultural land to be purchased.
b.
The locaticn, lot number, class and size of the land
to be purchased:
Consideration:
d. Time of urchase:
If any

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
- 5 -
If any person who is entitled to ownership is dissatisfied
with regard to the decision of the Agricultural Land Commission
he may file complaints to the said Commission during the period
of time for inspection mentioned above.
If the Agricultural Land Commission receives the complaints
mentioned above, it will make a decision within twenty days
after the inspection period.
The applicant who is not satisfied with the decision
mentioned in the preceding paragraph may file a petition with
the Agricultural Land Commission of the Prefecture concerned
within ten days of the decision.
If the above-said Agricultural Land Commission receives
the petition mentioned in the preceding paragraph, it will make
a ruling within twenty days from the day on which the period
of time mentioned in the latter part of the preceding paragraph
has expired.
The purchase will ultimately be effected by means of
delivery of the writ of purchase by the prefectural governor
to the owner of the said land. In cases where the writ cannot
be delivered to the owner by reason of being unknown or other-
wise a public notice of the matter is served.
6. Payment by the Government to the owner.
Payment by the Government for the land urchasedconsists
of consideration and subsidy. The amount of the consideration
for each lot to be  urchased is decided by the Commission
within the limit of the aaount which represents forty (40)
times the rental velue for paddy field and forty-eight (48)
times for upland field, thus usually giving the owner about
seven hundred and sixty yen ¥ 760) per one "Tan" (one-tenth of
one "chobu") of paddy field and four hundred and fifty yen
($ 45C) for upland field, while the amount of subsidy is
decided by the Government with the standard of two hundred and
twenty yen (¥ 220) for paddy field and one hundred and thirty
yen (¥ 130) for upland field per one "Tan" each.
The owner can receive the payment in each up to four
thousand yen ($ 4,000), but the rest of the amount is paid by
annual instalments with interests of 3.2% per year within the
period of thirty years.
7. Conclusion
It is presumed that the Japanese residents abroad heretofore
have had their proxies or custodians for their properties in
Japan, who may now be expected to take all the necessary steps
in their behalf for safeguarding their interests. If, however,
they find themselves unable to rest thus reassured, they may
directly address to the Agricultural Land Commission (Nochi-
Iin-Kai) of the City, Town or Village where their farm lands
are

situated. It goes without saying that the Ministry of Foreign

.EMORANDUM FOR:

Central Liaison Office, Tokyo.
SUBJECT
Rural Land Reform.
1. In order that the Imperial Japanese Government shall
remove economic obstacles to the revival and strengthening
of democratic tendencies, establish respect for the dignity of
man and destroy the economic bondage which has enslaved the
Japanese farmer for centuries of feudal oppression, the Japanese
Iaperial Government is directed to take measures to insure that
those who till the soil of Japan shall have a more equal
opportunity to enjoy the fruits of their labor.
2. The purpose of this order is to exterminate those
permicious ills which have long blighted the agrarian struc-
ture of a land where almost half the total population is
engaged in husbandry. The more malevolent of these ills include.
a. Intense overcrowding of land. Almost half the farm
households in Japan till less than one and one half
acres each.
b. Widespread tenancy under conditions highly unfavorable
to tenants.
More than three-fourths of the farmers in Japan are
either partially or totally tenants, paying rentals
amounting to half or more of their annual crops.
C. A heavy

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 4 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures
to Japanese Subjects Residing Abroad".
-7 -
c. A heavy burden of farm indebtedness combined with
high rates of interest on farm loans.
Farm indebtedness persists so that less than half
the total farm households are able to support themselves
on their agriculture income.
d. Governaent fiscal policies w ich discriminate against
agriculture in favor of industry and trade.
Interest rates and direct taxes on agriculture are
more oppressive than those in conmerce and industry.
e. Authoritative government control over farmers and
farm organization Without regard for farmer interests.
Arbitrary crop quotas established by disinterested
control associations often restrict the farmer in the
cultivation of crops for his own needs or economic
advancement.
Emancipation of the Japanese farmer cannot begin until
such basic farim evils are uprooted and destroyed.
3. The Japanese Imperial Government is therefore ordered
to submit to this head uarters on or before 15 March 1946
a program of rural 1und reform. This program shall contain plans
for:
a. Transfer of land ownership from absentee land owners
to land operators.
b. Provisions for urchase of farm lands from non-operating
owners at equitable rates.
c. Provisions for tenant urchase of land at annual
installments comensurate with tenant income.
d. Provisions for reasonable protection of former tenants
against reversion to tenancy status. Such necessary safe-
guards should include:
(1)
Access to long and snort term farm credit at
reasonable interest rates.
(2)
Measures to protect the farmer against exploitation
by processors and distributors.
(3)
Measures to stabilize prices of agricultural produce.
(4)
Plans for the diffusion of technical and other
information of assistance to the agrarian population.
(5)
A pro ram to foster and encourage an agricultural
cooperative movement free of domination by nonagrarian
interests and dedicated to the economic and cultural
advancement of the Japanese farmer.
e. The

Enclosure No. 4 to Despatch No. 1118 dated June 17, 1947 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject, "Application of Japanese Rural Land Reform Measures

-8 -
e. The Jaanese Imperial Government is requested to
subait in addition to the above, such other proposals it deems
necessary to guarantee to agriculture a share of the national
income commensurate with its contribution.

Subject: Applicability of Japanese
Agrarian Reform Laws to

From: Legal Section
To: Natural Re-
Date: 16 May 1947
sources
Section
Note No. 1
I. PROBLEM:
The Natural Resources Section has requested an opinion
as to whether the Imperial Japanese Government may expro-
priate farm lands in Japan belonging to American citizens
pursuant to general lerislation on social reforms, appli-
cable alike to nationals and aliens, if compensation for
the expropriation is payable to such citizens in Yen cur-
rency of a presently unkno:m international monetary value.
II. FACTS:
1. The Japanese Government, embarking on an agrarian
land reform program, enacted on 11 October 1946, the Owner-
Farmer Establishment Special Measures Law and the Agricul-
tural Land Adjustment Law. Both of these laws, favorably
considered by SCAP, were promulgated by the Japanese Govern-
ment on 21 October 1946.  The laws provided for the purchase
by the Japanese Government of agricultural land, owmed by
absentee owners and corporations whose principal business
was other than cultivation, and also land owned in excess of
certain specified holdings. The average price to be paid to
the land owmers ranges from approximately Yen 446 to Yen 757
per 1/4 acre. The purpose of these enactments was to mini-
mize farm tenancy by enabling tenants to purchase the land
they cultivate and to guarantec proper conditions of tenancy
to those who continue to farm as tenants.
2. In support of the Japanese laws, from an internation-
al law point of view it is claimed that:
a. Since the Japanese statutes do not discriminate
either for or against oimers or tenants on the grounds of
race, nationality, creed, or political opinion, and,
b. Since American citizens legally acquiring Japan-
ese agricultural lands prior to the enactment of the land
reform laws did so with the knowledge, express or implied,
that property owmed in Japan was subject to any laws enacted
by the Japanese Government, and,
RESTRICTED
/c. Since

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 2 of Enclosure No. 1 to
Despatch No. 41l dated June
RESTRTCTED
17, 1947, from Yokohama, Japan.
c. Since the statutes do not confiscate the land
but provide for payment in Yen, which is far in excess of
prices prevailing during the period the land was acquired
by such citizens,
that, therefore in view of these factors, all agricultural
lands within the territorial jurisdiction of the Japanese
Government are, regardless of the nationality of their own-
ers, subject to agrarian land reform laws.
3. If this contention is true, American absentee land
owners will be deprived of their property and receive as
compensation therefor a currency with no present foreign
exchange value. The Legal Section does not believe that the
payment in Yen under the circunstances suffices to make an
othervwise illegal confiscatory measure legal.
III. DISCUSSION:
4. a. Generally speaking, expropriation is the taking
of private property for public use provided compensation is
paid. It is similar to the right of eminent domain under
American law. It is the conpulsary taking of private prop-
erty from a person and is distinguishable from an act of
confiscation in that cxpropriation requires payment for such
property. As a well recognized principle of international
law, expropriation of foreign property obligates the State
to indennify foreign omers. This rule has been justified
by authoritics on the subject as based on natural law, sound
reason, equity, and justice. In any event, for the purposes
of a solution to the stated  ro lem it wili be assumed that
the Japanese Government was legally vithin its right to ex-
propriate farn land under its jurisdiction upon making pay-
ment therefor. The 1 roblem therefore resolves itself into
determining, not whether the Japanese Government could leg-
ally take farm land oimed by American nationals, but whether
in so taking it, the requirenents of compensation were met.
b. Nor coula the fact that the Supreme Commander's
favorable consideration of the statutes be interpreted as
sanctioning confiscatory legisletion, since it could not be
anticipated that a lawful act vould be unlavfully applied.
The real issue, therefore, is tc cetermine if payment in
Yen to American oimers of farm land in Japan, meets the re-
quirements set by international law.
5. a. A case practically identical in all respects to
the situation under iscussion arose betveen the United
States and Mexico vith recard to the Hexican expropriation
of agrarian properties which began in 1915. By 1938, the
United States pointed out to the Mexican Government that it
had not cid for any of the property taken from American
citizens end inquired of Mexico what its intentions were in
the natter. As a result of the exchange of notes between
the two governments on the subject there was crystalized
the United States position on the question of what consti-
tutes compensation for expropriated property taken from
American citizens.
/b. The
RESTRIOTED

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 3 of Enclosure No. 1 to
Despatch No. 411 dated June 17,
1947, from Yokohama, Japan
b. The United States contended that expropriation
unaccompanied by provision "for adequate, effective and
prompt payment for the property seized" is internationally
illegal. Mexico admitted that she was under an obligation
to indemnify in an adequate manner but that the time and
manner of such payment must be determined by her own laws.
The Mexican position was, in substance, that when an alien,
however lawfully, acquires land within Mexican territory,
he does so subject to the condition that the territorial
sivereign, when enbarked upon a policy of social betterment
through agrarian reform, may have recourse to expropriation
on such terms as it may devise, so long as they are impar-
tial in application as between nationals and aliens. It
was contended by Mexico that the future of the nation, when
committed to such a policy, may not be halted by the impos-
sibility of paying immediately the value of the properties
belonging to a small number of foreigners seeking "only a
lucrative end." (32 AJIL 759, Oct 1938)
c. In reply to this Mexican pretension, the United
States Secretary of State liull declared:
"Reduced to its essential terms, the contention
asserted by the Mexican Government, as set forth
in its reply and as evidenced by its practices
in recent years, is plainly this: That any gov-
ernment may on the ground that its municipal
legislation so permits, or on the plea that its
financial situation makes prompt and adequate
compensation onerous or impossible, seize prop-
erties omed by foreigners within its jurisdic-
tion, utilize them for whatever purpose it
sees fit, and refrain from providing effective
payment therefor, cither at the time of seizure
or any assured time in the future. I do not
hesitate to maintain that this is the first oc-
casion in the history of the Western Hemisphere
that such a theory has been seriously advanced.
In the opinion of my government, the doctrine so
proposed runs counter to the basic precepts of
international law and of the law of every Amer-
ican republic, as well as to every principle of
right and justice upon which the institutions
of the American republics are founded. It seems
to the Covernment of the United States a conten-
tion alien to the history, the spirit and the
ideals of democracy as practiced throughout the
independent life of all the nations of this con-
tinent."
6. a. In 1924 when the Rumanian Government declined to
give assurances that if property of American citizens in
Bessarabia should be expropriated adequate compensation would
be paid, the United States took the position that:
"Should property in Bessarabia belonging to Ameri-
can citizens be expropriated they would under the
generally accepted principles of international
law, be entitled to adequate compensation and
/that
RESTPIOTRD

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 4 of Enclosure No. 1 to
Despatch No. 411 dated June 17,
RBOTRIOTED
1947, from Yokohama, Japan.
that it is not necessary for this Government to
resort to any special agreement in order to ob-
tain for its nationals the treatment to which
they are justly entitled under the law of nat-
ions." (Hackworth Vol. I, p 21)
b. Also in 1922 a similar stand was taken by the
United States vhen it insisted that just compensation be
made for the expropriation by the Chinese authorities of
property omed by American citizens. (Hackworth Vol III
p 654)
7. a. The American Bar Association, in a study on this
problem stated:
"If therefore, a state contends that it may, in
an international sense, not improperly deprive
an alien of his lawfully acquired land within
its domain without full compensation when he has
had no reason to anticipate such a deprivation,
and when he would not have made his acquisition
had he been apprised of such a contingency, its
action is utterly lacking in the essentials of
good faith. For that reason, it places itself
outside the domain of international law when it
endeavors to vindicate such a course." (Hyde
Inter. Law, p 717)
b. The Fernanent Court of International Justice stat-
ed with respect to expropriation proceedings that: "Such a
measure leads to an international obligation to pay compen-
sation for the value taken," and, in another case, said
"lawful expropriation vould have brought about the obligation
to pay cash compensation for direct losses." (Am. Journ. of
Inter. Law, Apr 41, p 253).
8. a. There can be no question but that expropriation
of foreign property obligates the expropriator to indemnify
the foreign owmer. Japanese legislation recognizes this
principle and accordingly makes provision for compensation.
Nevertheless this Section doubts whether the Japanese pro-
visions for payment under the circumstances of the present
belligerent occupation meets the test of "adequate effective
and prompt" compensation as laid dowm by the United States
in the Mexican agrarian dispute. In that controversy the
American view that compensation, to be "adequate", was inter-
preted to mean that it should equalize the full loss sustain-
ed by the ovmer in consequence of the taking of the property
concerned. The time of payment was considered an important
factor because, if payment were deferred, the total amount
would fail to be fully compensatory if it did not make pro-
vision, among other things, for interest on the investment,
or for loss or benefits to the owmer after the property was
taken and prior to payment. (IHyde Inter. Law, p 718). How-
ever, more concrete is the rule that only full and immediate
compensation in cash fulfills the conditions of international
law. (Am. Journ. of Inter. Law, Apr 41, p 259).
/b. With

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 5 of Enclosure No. 1 to
Despatch No. 411 dated June 17,
1947, from Yokohama, Japan.
b. With respect to the question of compensation in
the Mexican controversy Secretary of State Hull informed
the Mexican Government that:
"The taking of property without compensation
is not expropriation. It is confiscation. It
is not less confiscation because there may be
an expressed intent to pay at some time in the
future. If it were permissible for a govern-
ment to take the private property of the cit-
izens of other countries and pay for it as and
when, in the judgment of that government, its
economic circunstances and its local legisla-
tion may perhaps permit, the safeguards which
the Constitutions of most countries and estab-
lished international law have sought to provide
would be illusory. Covernments would be free
to take property far beyond their ability or
willingness to pay, tnd the owners thereof
would be without recourse. We cannot question
the right of a foreign government to treat its
own nationals in this fashion if it so desires.
This is a matter of domestic concern. But we
cannot admit that a foreign government may take
the property of American nationals in disregard
of the rule of compensation under international
law. Nor can ve admit that any government uni-
laterally and throuch its municipal legislation
can, as in this instant case, nullify this uni-
versally accerted Irinciple of international
law, based as it is on reason equity and jus-
tice." (Haclworth Vo1.III. p 656)
c. And again in a lcter note to the Mexican Govern-
ment, the Secretary of State declared that:
"The Covernment of the United States merely
adverts to a self-evident fact when it notes
that the applicable precedents and recognized
authorities on international law support its
declaration that, under every rule of law and
equity, no overnnent is entitled to expropriate
private property, for whatever purpose, without
provision for pronpt, adequate, and effective
payment therefor. In addition, clauses appear-
ing in the constitution of the Anerican repub-
lics, ombody tho principle of just compensation.
Those, in hensclves, are declaratory of the
like principle in the law of nations." (Page
193 of Cfficial Docunents - AJIL Oct 38)
9. a. It has been sugcested that if the American theory
be duly respected, 
"torritorial sovereign may find its very right
to expropriate conditioned upon its power to
pay and that if it sought to exercise that
right vhen evidence of the possession of such
/power
RESTRESTED

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.98)
Page 6 of Enclosure No. 1 to
Despatch Ho. 41l dated June 17,
1947, from Yokohama, Japan.
power and the disposition to use it are not
evident, there is reason to demand there be
restored to the omers what may have been
taken from them." (Hyde Inter. Law, p 720)
b. Thus, if this be the consequence of the Japan-
ese agrarian land reform measure, its advocates may seek
consolation in the fact that modern social reforms under
unusually difficult economic conditions cannot and should
not be effected at the expense of a policy which would re-
sult in collapse of international economic and commercial
relations which might imperil the very foundations of civil-
ization. At least such was the opinion of the Secretary of
State in 1938. (32 AJIL, Oct 1938, 0ff. Doc. p 194).
10. a. Compensation in a derreciated yen currency is not
the fiscal equivalent of "adequate effective and prompt"
compensation or "just coupensation" in accordance with the
requirements set by international law to counterbalance the
fuil loss suffered by the absentee owmer. Payments in a
debased currency, as in the present case, would act to the
discrimination of, and financiel loss to an American ovmer.
When a debasement of currency hes resulted in financial
loss to its citizens, thc United States, in a case where
the Spanish Government attempted to discharge its debts
in a depreciated currency, stated that:
"The injustice of this is manifest. Between
discharging & delt by Taying one half of its
nominal tmount tnd the whole of its nominal
amount possessing only one helf its real value,
there is no difference." (Moore Vol VI, p 753)
b. The present problem roes even further than that
of payment in a depreciated currency in view of the fact
that the present Japanese Yen has no foreign exchange value.
The most that cen be said for the yen is that at some time
in the future it may be of some value. Such contingencies
do not meet the standerds of the international rule of law
for expropriation as enunciated by the United States.
Therefore, the taking of property ond discharging the in-
debtednesh for so talking by payment in a currency which is
of no value to the absentee owmer would be confiscation
and not expropriation. Payment in this currency would
create in the Japenese Government an additional purchasing
power and allow it to obtain possession of this property
at low prices to the detriment of the absentee owner.
IV. OFIHIOH:
11. It is the opinion of the Legal Section that the
proposed expropriation by the Imperial Japanese Government
of agrarian property owmed by American citizens which was
legally acquired prior to the enactment of the agrarian
land reform laws, by payment in Yen, would be confiscatory
and illegal.

ters of record prior to the enactment of such laws unless
other measures for compensation are adopted which will be
prompt, adequate, and effective.

From: NR/A

Date: 28 May 1947
ATTN: Yokohama Division
Note No. 2.
1. The question involving treatment by the Japanese
Government of certain agricultural lands owned by Mr.
George Tsutsui, an American citizen residing in Los Angeles,
California, arises in connection with the Japanese agrarian
reform program. Pertinent facts relating to this program
are as follows:
a. The Japanese agrarian reform program is based on
the Ovmer-Farmer Establishment Special Measures Law and the
Agricultural Land Adjustment Law as amended. Both measures
were favorably considered by General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, on 14 August 1946, enacted
by the Japanese Diet on 11 October 1946, and promulgated on
21 October 1946.
b. The purpose of the progran is twofold:
(1) To minimize farm tenancy by enabling tenants
to purchase the land they cultivate.
(2) To guarantee proper conditions of tenancy
to those who continue to farm as tenants.
c. With certain minor exceptions, the following
agricultural lands will be purchased by the Japanese Govern-
ment for resale to tenants:
(1) All cultivated lands owmed by persons whose
permanent residence vas not, on or before
23 November 1945, located in the city, town,
or village in which the land is located, or
in an adjacent city, towm, or village.
(2) All tenant operated agricultural land hold-
ings in excess of one cho (2.45 acres) per
landlord (four cho in Hokkaido).
(3) All lands operated by owmer-cultivators in
excess of three cho (twelve cho in Hokkaido).
This is inclusive of tenant operated lands
owmed by persons in this category.
(4) All agricultural lands owmed by corporations
whose principal object is other than culti-
vation and such other corporation owned
agricultural lands as are considered proper.
(5) Such uncultivated lands as are considered
capable of reclamation for purposes of culti-
vation.
/d. The

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 2 of Enclosure No. 2 to
Despatch No. 411 dated June 17,
RESTRICTED
1947, from Yokohama, Japan
d. The terms of purchase and resale are specified
by law.
e. The laws upon which the agrarian reform program
is based make no provision for discrimination either for or
against owners or tenants on the grounds of race, national-
ity, creed or political opinion.
2. The general problem regarding the effect of the
agrarian reform proram on lands owmed by allied nationals
is currently under study by NR and other interested sections
in GHQ, SCAP. Although a final decision has not been made
on this matter it is the opinion of all sections concerned
that where allied nationals acquire owmership of Japanese
agricultural land subsequent to the date of promulgation of
the agrarian reform legislation (21 October 1946) such par-
ties take title with knowledge, either express or implied,
of the agrarian reform laws and any owmership rights ac-
quired are subject to those laws. Attention is invited to
the inclosed opinion of LS, SCAF, concerning the instant
case.
3. IR recommends that Ir. George Tsutsui be notified
that his lands are subject to the laws upon which the agra-
rian reform program is based.
4. Notification of final action taken on this case is
requested.
5. Concurrences:

Sir:
Reference is made to your letters of March 13, March
24, and March 28, 1947, regarding the transfer of property
in Japan from Manso Tsutsui to George Tsutsui, from Iohie
Kono to Akiko Kono Matauura, and from Fusakichi Notowaki
to Tai Motowaki, respectively, the transferor in each case
being a Japanese national and the transferee an American
national.
As the registrations of the transfers of these par-
cels of land constitute a legal service which, in accord-
ance with the Foreign Service Regulations of the United
States, this office is not authorized to perform, all of
the documents enclosed in the letters under reference are
being returned.
However, in order to be of assistance to you in this
matter inquiry was made to determine whether it would be
possible for the transfer to be effected in any other
manner.
Under directive no. 45 of September 22, 1945, from
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers to the Japan-
ese Government, all transactions involving property lo-
cated in Japan owned or controlled by any person outside
of Japan are prohibited without the express approval of
the Supreme Commander. This office has been informed by
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, that it does not consider that sufficient justi-
fication has been shown to fustify the approval at this
time of exceptions to this policy in these cases.
It is

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers. that where
Allied nationals acquire ownership of Japanese agri-
cultural land subsequent to the promulgation of the
agrarian reform legislation on October 21, 1946, such
parties take title with the knowledge, either express
or implied, of the agraricn reform laws, and any own-
ership rights so acquired are subject to those laws.
Therefore, even if the transfer of title frem Japanese
to American ownarship in these cases were to be effeated
with the approval of the Supreme Commander, any owmer-
ship rights so acquired would be subject to the provi-
sions of the agrarian land reform measures.
Very truly yours,
U. Alexis Johnson,

My dear Mr. Nakahara:
Reference is mde to your letter of Ootober 1, 1947, concerning
the applicability of the Japanese rural land referm measures to certain
894.52/
property owned in Japan by your father, whieh you state your family has
inherited sinoe his death.
Generally speaking, the Japanese rural land reform measures apply to
all Japanese nationals whether they are residing in Japan or abroad.
The question of whether such measures apply to property owmed by Uaited
21
Nations' nationals, is presently before General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers in Japan. Pending determination of this
question, the disposal under these measures of such property is being
held in abeyance.
There is enclosed for your information, a copy of "A Brief Explan-
ation About the Farm Land Reform Programmse for Japanese Nationals Abroad"
which was prepared by the Japanese Ministry of Foreign Affairs. It is
believed that this dooument will fully answer the questions you have
raised in your letter under reference.

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 104 dated February 16, .1948
from the Office of the United States Political Adviser at
Tokyo on the subject of: "Implementation of Rural Land Reform
Program."
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
1330
Press Release:
10 February 1948
SCAP DIRECTS JAPAMESE GOVERNMENT TO TAKE PROMPT ACTION AGAINST
PERSONS WHO OBSTRUCT IMPLEMENTATION OF LAND REFORM MEASURES.
To put an end to delays which landlords in some localities have
caused in the sale of their land to the Japanese, Government, SCAP
today directed the Japanese Government to "take prompt and vigorous
action" against persons who "obstruct the implementation of land
reform measures."
SCAP ordered the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry to "in-
struct prefectural and local agricultural land commissions to
purchase without delay all land subject to land reform law...with-
out regard to unauthorized action by pressure organizations seeking
to impede the objectives of the land reform program."
The Japanese land reforin progran enacted by the Japanese Diet
Oct. 21, 1946 has moved ahead smoothly with the cooperation of all
concerned in most parts of Japan, SCAP Agricultural officials ex-
plained. By the end of 1947, they said 3,460,719 acres (1,424,453
cho) were acquired by the Japanese Government from landlords for
resale to tenants. At least 71 percent of all cultivated land to
be purchased under the program has already been acquired, they
pointed out. In several places, however, landlords have sought to
obstruct, delay or evade the purposes of the program, they said.
The directive to the Japanese Government points out that the
land reform laws were enacted in accordance with a SCAP directive
of December 9, 1945, "in order to eliminate the feudal system of
land tenure and remove economic obstacles to the redistribution of
the land on an equitable and democratic basis. Since the enactment
of these land reform laws, however, efforts have been made by
certain adversely affected interests to obstruct the accomplishments
of the rural land reform program."
The "firm implementation" of the program, the directive con-
tinues, "is essential to the creation in Japan of a society which
is truly free and democratic and, as a consequence, it has become
one of the foremost objectives of the Japanese people as well as of
the Allied occupation. Therefore, the strict, vigorous and fearless
enforcement of the above-mentioned laws is both imperative and
indispensable."
"The directive should serve notice to dissident groups that
the laws will be enforced, and that equitable distribution of the
land will be carried out," SCAP officials said.
They added that SCAP is "fully behind all branches of the
Japanese Government in any way connected with adninistration and
enforcement of the land reform laws."
Japanese

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
No. 104, February 16, 1948.
- 2 -
Japanese Agriculture and Forestry Ministry officials dealing
with land reform enforcement have reported to SCAP that feudalis-
tic relationships persisting between landlord and tenant have
occasionally resulted in intimidation of a number of tenants who
would like to purchase land, but who feel they are in too weak a
position to demand their rights under the land reform program.
These feudal relationships are reported to have affected
decisions of some land commission members by intimidation to favor
landlords over the tenants. In other instances, law enforcement
officials have hesitated to incur the displeasure of powerful land-
lord groups, it was pointed out.
The directive, the first from SCAP on land reform since the
original directive calling for land distribution in l945, aims at
facilitating a system of independent owner-farmers in Japan. The
1945 directive ordered the Japanese Government "to insure that
those who till the soil shall have a more equal opportunity to
enjoy the fruits of their labor."

and the Agricultural Land Adjustment Law were enacted in accordance
with the reference memorandum, in order to eliminate the feudal
system of land tenure and remove economic obstacles to the re-
distribution of the land on an equitable and democratic basis.
Since the enactment of these land reform laws, however, efforts
have been made by certain adversely affected interests to ob-
struct the accomplishment of the rural land reform profram.
3. The firm implementation of the Land Reform Program is
ossential to the creation in Japan of a society which is truly
free and democratic and, as a consequence, it has become one of
the foremost ob jectives of the Japanese people as well as of the
Allied occupation. Therefore, the strict, vigorous and fearless
enforcement of the above-mentioned laws is both imperative and
ind ispensable.

prefectural and local agricultural land commissions to purchase
without delay all land subject to Land Reform Law in accordance
with existing procedures and without regard to unauthorized action
by pressure organizations seeking to impede the objectives of the

nationals, 1,000 cho by Axis nationals, 450 cho by persons having both

894.5/7-648
4. On July 18, 1947, the Japanese Government requested the ap-
proval of this Headquarters, as it was required to do by SCAPIN 45 of

5. SCArIN 19ll constitutes the first decision by this Head-

Government
RESTRICTED

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No. 414,
July 6, 1948.
-2-
Government should be permitted to apply the land reform program to
United Nations nationals, although this matter has been under care-
ful consideration for over one year. The problem is posed by the
apparent irreconcilability, on the one hand, of the provisions of a
basic directive from the United States Government to the Supreme Com-
mander requirin him to preserve and protect the property of United
Nations nationals and to impound or block property owned by absentee
owners of non-Japanese nationality (JCS directive No. 1380/15, para-
graphs 8e and 45), and, on the other hand, of the purposes of the
land reforn progran in Japan as directed by the Supreme Commander in
SCArIN 411 of December 9, 1945, and as instituted by the Japanese
Government in the Law Joncerning Special Measures for Establishment
of Ovmer Farmers. Major disagreement within this Headquarters over
several issues raised by this problem has delayed solution.
6. The first of these issues was the matter of compensation to
absentee owners if their property were purchased and disposed of by
the Japanese Government in accordance with the land reform law. This
issue is fully discussed in the enclosures to despatch no. 4ll of
June 17, 1947 from the Yokohama Branch of this Mission to the Depart-
ment. As indicated in despatch no. 4'1, it was proposed to refer the
entire problem to ashington for a solution but this proposal in turn
met with no general agreement and hence no decision was reached.
During the discussion it was pointed out that on June 19, 1947, the
Far astern Connission had decided in Fac-Ol4/9, Part II, paragraph
3, that where protection of United Mations interests conflicts with
the fulfillment of the objectives of the Cecupation, the government
of the nation concerned should oe inforned through diplomatic channels
and should be consulted on the question of proper adjustments; this
broad policy determination was not c nsidered applicable in the present
instance.
7. The Japanese Government on its own initiative indicated in
June 1947 that it would apply the land reform program to Japanese
nationals whether in Japan or abroad and, presumably, regardless of
dual nationality. This principle has now been incorporated in SCAPIN
1911 of June 19, 1948. Information on this statement of position by
the Japane se lovernment was Porwarded to the Department by this
Hission's despatch no. 1113, June 17, 1947, and despatch no. 1143,
June 29, 1947. The Japane se Governnent has not, however, actually ap-
plied the provisions of the land reforn law to Japanese nationals
abroad or to any omers or land who were not resident in Japan.
8. After it had heen generally agreed that United Nations nation-
als should be exempted, at least until a foreign exchange rate for the
Japanese yen was established, a second major issue was raised in con-
nection with the applicability of the land reform law to domestic
religious orders. The Civil Iroperty Custodian, who is charged with
primery responsibility in protecting the interests of United Nations
nationals, was of the informal opinion that the land reform program
should not be applied to property held by religious orders, many of
which are financed by foreign nationals. In opposition, the Government
Section of this Headquarters took the following position: "It is the
opinion of the Government ection that nothing could do more harn to
the land reform program than to exempt from the land reform law prop-
erty of religious orders, whether the funds for the purpose of such
property were originally provided by United Nations nationals or enemy,
neutral, or indigenous nationals, especially when the legal and bene-
ficial ownership of such property is vested in a domestic corporation
sole
RLSTRICTED

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No.'414,
July 6, 1948.
-3-
sole, such as the Bishop of Fukuoka. More than one violent revolu-
tion in the history of the modern world can be traced to the resent-
ment of the peasantry engendered by disproportionately large land
holdings of church authorities. Such exenption would inevitably
create conditions conducive to the development of communistic tend-
encies and h nd on a silver platter to the Communist Party of Japan
an issue which, historically popular in the western world, would be
even more sympathetically received in a non-Christian, oriental
country such as Japan." The property involved in controversy is
mainly the agricultural lands owned by the Roman Catholic Diocese of
Fukuoka, and although the Suprene Coanander in a letter dated March
4, 1947, to the Connanding General of the United States Eighth Army,
had stated that agricultural lands owmed by this diocese are subject
to the provisions of the land reform laws, it appears that this issue
is still unresolved.
9. SCAr IN 191l was approved by the Supreme Commander's Chief of
Staff on June 14, 194ε, ith the instruction to the Civil Property
Custodian that "mestions of interpretation of the application of the
proposed SCA IN and of the Japanese Agrarian Land Reform Law in spe-
cial cases such as land owned by religious organizations, land owned
by commercial interests, and land owmed by individuals of dual nation-
ality, will be considered separately. Cases not falling within policy
already established will be submitted to the Chief of Staff for final
decision." It would sean likely that many individual cases involving
interpretation of CAr IN 19ll will arise and will require decision by
the Chief of Staff.
10. It has been learned that the Japanese Government will not
actually apply the provisions of SCA. IN 19ll to land owned by United
Nations nationals or by persons outside Japan until October 1948. It
is hoped that preliminary problems of interpretation will have been
resolved by that time. This Mission considers that the provision of
SCArIN 191l, exenpting from the land reform law all land owned by
"nationals of one of the United Nations or Neutral Nations who do not
in addition possess Japanese nationality," is of particular interest
to the Department, especially as it applies to persons in the United
States possessing both American and Japanese nationality. This pro-
vision was inserted in the directive in response to the following in-
struction by the Chief of Staff: "The Japanese Government will be
authorized to purchase land owned by: . . .(2) Persons of Japanese
descent, not Japanese nationals but claiming dual nationality, part of
which is Japanese, residing abroad."
11. Several residual questions raised by SCAPIN 191l, such as the
disposition of former Axis-owned land and of land held by United
Nations nationals not also possessing Japanese nationality, also
remain for future determination.

SUBJECT:
Purchase and Resale of Agricultural Lands Owned by
Persons of Dual Nationality
1. Reference is made to memorandum for the Japanese Government,
file AG 004 (19 Jun 48)CPC/FP, SCAFIN 1911, 19 June 1943, subject,
"Application for General Permission to Dispose of Properties in Japan
Owned by Persons Outside of Japan ccording to Law Concerning Special
Leasures for Establishment of Owner-Farmer," from General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander. for the Allicd Powers.
2. In implementing refcrence 1 above, the Japanese Government is
authorized to consider as Japanose nationals persons so registered in the
records of Japan.
3. In cases in which there is doubt as to the correctness of the
records with regard to dotermining nationality, the land may be pur-
chased by the Japanese Government but will not be disposed of at this
time. This procodure will afford an opportunity for tho owner to submit
proof that he does not possess Japanoso nationality. When such proof is
submitted, the purchase of the land will be cancelled.
4. The Japanese. Governmont will notify owners when their land is
purchased. A report of the procedure to be followed in notification of
the owners will be furnished Goneral Headquarters, Supreme Commander for
the Alliod Powers within fifteen (15) days of the date of this memorandum.

The Secretary of State,
