Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch No. 414,
894.
ACTION
July 6, 1948 regarding application of the Japanese land reform pro-
FE
gram to United Nations nationals and to land owners outside of Japan.
INFO
In this connection there are enclosed copies of a directive
5
DCR
from this Headquarters to the Japanese Government (SCAPIN 191l/1,
2
L
December 4, 1948) authorizing the Japanese Government to treat as
12-1648
ITP
Japanese nationals persons so registered in the records of Japan and
OFD
to notify owners when their land is purchased in accordance with the
0
land reform program. It is al so provided that owmers shall have the
opportunity to prove that they do not possess Japanese nationality.
As stated in despatch no. 414, it has been decided that the land
reform program should apply to persons of dual nationality one of
which is Japanese.
The Japanese Government has been instructed to submit a report
to this Headquarters describing the procedure it intends to follow
in notifying owners of the purchase of their land. In the case of
owners in Japan it is anticipated that the Japanese Government will
netify them directly by mail or by publicity in the press concerning
the sale of their land.
The procedure for notifying persons out side of Japan, however,
has not yet been decided. Existing SCAP regulations prohibit
communications by the Japanese Government to points outside of Japan
and furthermore no Japanese are permitted to send abroad "papers of
legal procedures". This Mission has taken the poaition, in the
absence of a change in policy regarding Japanese foreign relations,
that, because of the legal and diplomatic problems connected with the
land reform program, communications from the Japanese Government to
dual nationals or to presuned Japanese nationals resident abroad
should be transmitted through this Headquarters. This position will
be asserted when the time comes for a decision by this Headquarters in
regard to the procedure for notifying owners abroad.
An ancillary problem now being considered by this lleadquarters
is that of the procedure to be followed in allowing the Japanese
RESTRICTED
Government

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No. 786
December 16, 1948
RESTRICTED
-2-
Government to correspond with Japanese nationals abroad who wdsh to
renounce their Japanese citizenship. Several Japanese-Americans in
the United States have, in letters to the Japanese Government or to
this Headquarters, indicated a desire to renounce their dapanese
nationality, in one or two cases as a result of the hope that they
can thereby prevent the application of the land reform program to
their property in Japan.
Existing SCAP regulations, as noted above, prohibit communica-
tions on such matters by the Japanese Government to points abroad.
It is likely that this Headquarters will decide to permit communica-
tions between the Japanese Government and Japanese abroad in regard to
renunciation of Japanese nationality, although such renunciation at
this time would not affect the application of the land reform program
to land in Japan.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 90 of
February 11, 1949 from the United
UNCLASSIFIED
States Political Adviser for Japan,
Tokyo, subject: "Return of Personal
Property to Members of Philippine
Puppet Government."
COPY
Press Release by General Headquarters, February 4, 1949:
VALUABLE JEWELS RETURNED TO OWNERS
Jewels claimed by the Philippine government to be the property
of Mrs. Jose R. Laurel, wife of the former president of the puppet
government of the Philippines, were to be turned over Friday to the
Philippine government, it was announced today by Brig. Gen. Patrick
H. Tansey, SCAP's Civil Property Custodian.
An official statement as follows mas made by the Chief of Staff:
"SCAP has approved, upon identification, restoration of personal
property of Mrs. Jose P. Laurel and her five children, and Mrs.
Bonigno Aquino, Mr. and Mrs. Camilo Osias and Mr. George D. Vargas."
The restitution will include only the property of the Laurel
and Osias families. Personal properties of Vargas and Aquino do
not include jewels, and will be returned at a later date. Among
the valuables to be returned will be at least a dozen diamond rings,
one of which weighs in excess of eight karats. Several seven-karat
diamond rings are also included. Insured value of the jewels was
approximately$100,000.
Former President Laurel served as leader of the Philippine
government during the Japanese occupation and in May 1945 he and his
family left the Philippines and fled to Formosa. Following the
liberation of the Philippines, Ex-president Laurel was exonerated
in a people's court of having collaborated with the Japanese. Vargas,
who was ambassador from the Philippines to Japan during Japan's occu-
pation of the islands, was also cleared of collaboration charges.
The Philippine court which granted amnesty to the pair did so on the
grounds that while members of the government during Japan's occupation,
they acted under duress.
The jewels which are being returned to the Philippine government
were the property of the Laurel and Osias families when they fled the
Philippines, according to statements made by the present Philippine
government which has repeatedly sought the return of the jewels on
the basis that they are possessions of Philippine citizens.
Counter Intelligence Corps agents originally took possession of
the jewels.
UNCLASSIFIED

The Department of State has been requested by the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Fowers in Japan, on behalf of the Japanese
Government, to give appropriate publicity in the United States and
elsewhere to the purchase of agricultural lands in Japan owmed by
Japanese residents abroad.
Since the Japanese Government is about to purchase agricultural
lands in Japan owned by Japanese residents abroad, it is desirous
of making known universally among Japanese nationals residing overseas
the facts in this connection as explained in the attachment to this
memorandum.
The Department is transmitting this information to various
governments. It is suggested that appropriate publicity might be
given the matter in the United States through a press release
based on the attachment herewith, as well as through letters which
this Division proposes to send to the editors of leading Japanese
language papers in the United States.

Subject: Agrarian Reform during the Occupation of Japan
Since sending members of the economic divisions copies of the en-
closed paper, "Japan: Occupation Period: Agrarian Reform", I have had
the benefit of Messrs. Dickover's, Williams', Johansen's and others!
criticisms, which have led me to revise my views somewhat. I hope that
your group will be able to consider this paper shortly in the light of
the following changes and clarifications:
1. There should be no attempt at comprehensive agrarian reform
in the immediate post-hostilities period, ie. probably for six months
394.52/6-149
or more. Only after a fair measure of economic stability has been
achieved, and food production and distribution have been restored to
satisfactory working order should the possibility of instituting a
reform program be considered. This may mean the dissipation of part
of the spontaneous pressure for reform likely to exist among the farm-
ing class, which pressure will probably be greatest immediately before
and following surrender, but this would seem unavoidable. During this
emergency period, however, the military authorities should, where pos-
sible, avoid suppressing spontaneous efforts of the farmers in their
own behalf and, consistent with the effective production and dastribution
2
ED
1949
C
1
164101

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
1-a~
of foodstuffs, should not enforce regular payments of rents, interest, and
other obligations should the peasantry refuse to meet these obligations.
2. At the end of the emergency period the occupation authorities
should take note of any spontaneous movements for agrarian reform appear-
ing among the Japanese farming classes (led, possibly, by university
professors, government officials, political leaders and others, in addi-
tion to local or provincial farm leaders) and of the attitude of other
classes of the population in the matter. If there is a reform movement
of substantial proportions which is sympathetically received, or at
least is not strongly opposed by other classes in the population, ex-
cept the landlords, the military government, with the approval of the
appropriate authorities in Washington,

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.97)
-2
should afford it its encouregement and support. Sush
encouragement and support wight bo in the nature of e
"green light" to Japanese individuals and orgenizations
pressing the cause of sgrarian reform to draw up a
reform progrem to be initiated with the approval and
under the authority of military government.
3. In order that the ocoupation muthorities may be
in a position properly to appraise, and, if its advise is
sought, guide the preparation of the reform progrsm
presented to it for approval end eupport a tentative
reform program should be drayn up by the state, Agrieulture
and, poesibly, other Government departments and ageneles
for use at the time. This progrem, when completed, would
not receive formel Government approval, but would samply
be held in readiness until its proper use, if any, could
be determined during the ocoupation period.
4. Japanese leaders desirous of instituting a pro-
gram of agrarian reform would be told during the sbeve
mentioned emergenoy period that a program whioh appeared
to be widely supported by the Japanese people would
receive the blessing and aupport of the military government.
My peraonal preference would be for the militery
government to assume greater initiative in the matter.
However, the important thing for the present, as I see
1t, 1e that the problem be thoroughly studied by the
sppropriate agencies of the Government and that we be
prepared for whatever couree may be deoided upon later.
I therefore propose that the above prinoiples, which
have been supported by thoee with whom I have disoussed
the matter in recent weeks, be aocepted as the basis of
approach to the problem.
Mr. Ladejinsky's Civil Affaire Guide "Food and Agri-
oulture Program for Japan" was approved with certein
changes at a meeting in the Agrioulture Depertment last
Monday, May 28. It was agreed however, that the seetion
in the guide on land reform in Japan during the ocoupation
period should be deleted. This was decided, however.
with Mr. Ladejineky's approval, only efter I hmd aseured
the Committee that it was my underetanding that the ques-
tion of whether the matter of agrarien reform during the
occupation period might profitably be studied in the
Government was under consideration in the Department, and
that a decision would be reeched as soon ae possible.

FOREWORD
During November and December of 1948, Dr. Arthur F. Raper,
Bureau of Agricultural Economics, U. S. Department of Agricul-
ture, in conjunction with the Natural Resources and the Civil
Information and Education Sections of General Headquarters, Su-
preme Commander for the Allied Powers, conducted a survey of 13
rural villages in Japan.
The purpose of the survey was primarily to assess changes
-- particularly those concerning the effects of tne land reform
program -- which had taken place in the villages since a simi-
lar survey made in May and June of 1947. A forthcoming report
will present tie general findings of the 1948 survey.
The present report contains only the materials obtainod
from a questionnairo submitted to hoads of farm houscholds in
the villages. It is boing published in advanco of the completo
report so that Military Government Teams, SCAP staff sections,
and othor organizations having an oporational or a policy mis-
sion may have these findings at thoir disposal as soon as pos-
sible. For a detailed description of lifo in the villages, and
for final conclusions and recommondations, the reader is ro-
ferred to tho forthcoming report.

INTRODUCTION
During a survey of 13 rural villages throughout Japan1 conducted in
November and ecember, 1948, a questionnaire was given to a sample of the
heads of farm households in each village. The sample was not intended to
be a representative cross section of the population of rural communities
throughout Japan, but only a sample of the heads of farm households2 of

Because of time and staff limitations, the size of the sample in
each village was limited to approximately 100 rosponients. Exactly 100
heads of farm households were selected in oach village, regardless of
the total number of farm housoholds. These individuals were solected
from the Land Commission olection registry for 1946. These:registries
contain information concerning all farmers in a village: the size of
their family, the size of thoir holdings, the amount of land ronted to
or from others, and the location of their home. They were used in this
study is the basis for select ing the sample partly because they are
completo, accurate, and acccssible, but largely because they contain
informntion upon which a sampie based upon tonure status prior to the
land reform could be dram. The l946 cntries in those rogistries were
made curront as of the date of tho sample, i e., death and other por-
sonal c'anges not related to land roform were taken into consideration.
The sarple, thorofore, contains landlords. tonants, and owaer cultivators
in prop rtion to their rumbars in Septomoor 94, and was so constructed
in prd to reflect the reacuions of each group to the Land voform pro-
gram.
To csh village the 100 casos were selected oy the teehnigue of
stratif ed random sampling. That is, they vere seioited ranooaly from
the registry in propertion to tho number of landlords, tenants, and
owner-cultivators in onch by csu as os 1945. J. is believed that this
method of sampling has reouited in a sampiing orror ranging between
one end seven percent per village, depenling upon the proportion of
responsos in any given answer category.
School toachers wero trained in each village to cdminister the
questionnaire. The individuals seloctod for tho sample camo to the
school building or the village office and filled out the questionnaire
themselves. A small proportion of cases in each village sample were
interviowed directly by the rosearchers in ordor to test the goneral
validity and meanings of responses. Respondents were asked not to
reveal thoir names and were urged to be completely frank and honest in
their responses.
Limitations of space do not permit the inclusion of all of the
survoy data obtained. Only those tabulations which seom to have the
most significance have been included. For purposes of uniformity,
all "no rosponse" answers hove been excluded from the porcentage cel-
culations, unless stated otherwise. Similarly, all represontntions
by tenure status and nll references to tonure status are on the basis
of pre-land roform tenure status, unloss othorvise st-tod. This hcs
boen dono for two rensons. First, it results in a more accurate analy-
sis of the opinions and reactions of the tonure groups most affected
by the land reform program, the pre-land reform tenants and landlords,
and second, it is a ropresentation which reflects the criteria used in
the sample design.

It should be noted that the terms used to describe tenure status in
this report may not always correspond to the commonly accepted definitions
of the words, but rather refer to the definitions used in land reform legis-
lation.4 A tenant is defined as a person engaging in cultivation who either
owns no agricultural land or who cultivates.at least twice as much land as
he owns; a landlord as a person who does not cultivate the land he owms or
who owms at least twice as much land as he cultivates; and an owmer-cultivator
as a person who cultivates, but who does not belong in either of the previous
categories.
The number of individuals or items represented by each l00 percent has
been designated by the symbol "n."
Because of the scope of t e survey of rural villages, the questionnaire
contained a wide variety of questions. Some of them were intended to secure
factual material concerning the lives of the farmers, and others to obtain
opinions and attitudes. In this analysis the questions have been subsumed
under eight chapter headings. Each chapter contains a brief background
statement, and an analysis of the responses to the questions which pertain
to the subject of tne chapter. Space limitations have prohibited the pre-
sentation of all of t e tabular materials, and only those representations
which bear most directly upon the subject under discussion have been ineluded.
However, representations of the data which might conflict vith the general
interpretations made have not been excluded; the materials which have been
excluded are those which offer only supplementary ovidenco.
4. Agricultural Land Adjustment Law, Law Number 42, 22 October 1946.

R E S T R I C T E D
SUMMARY
General Economic Probloms of Farmers
There is evidence of considerable and widespread uneasiness among all
classes of farmers concerning their curront economic situation and their
prospects for the near future. A majority of all farmers felt that their
living conditions had worsened in tho last year. Income taxes, the clothing
shortago, and the general high prices are frequent sources of hardship.
Most of t e farmers cither dofinitely expect an agricultural depression or
are afraid that one may come.
Use of Appeal Procedures
A majority of the farm rs in all tenure groups vere avare of the fact
that appeal procedures are available. More farmors knew of income tax
appeal procedures then knew of grain quota appeal prooedures, and about
one-quarter of the farmers actually had mede an appeal. There was great
variation in the villnge-by-village results, reflecting local conditions,
but in 10 of the 13 villages, the number of appeals to the income tax as-
sessmonts wns significantly and in some cases markodly higher than the
number of appenls to grain quotas.
Attitudes Tovard the Land Reform Program
One-half of all t:e farmers and over two-thirds of the tonants folt
that the land reform program had benefited their families, and more than
one-half of all the farm rs and exactly two-thirds of the tenants felt that
the program had been beneficial to their village. The program was generally
unpopular among landlorus. Fifty percent of all the farmers felt that land-
lords should not bo allowed to retain land other than that which they cul-
tivate thcmselves. While the general level of satisfaction with the land
reform program seoms to be high, many specific complaints were voiced by
farmers. The most frequently mentioned points of dissatisfaction wore that
landlords should not have been permitted to retain so much land cultivated
by othors, that land purchaso was unduly complex, and that forest, meadow,
and unreclaimed land should have been included in the program.
Landlord-Tenant Relations
At the time of tie survey, cight percent of all farmers renting land
from others reported that they had a written tenancy contract for all of
the rented land, 22 pereent reported that they had a contract for part,
and 70 percent reported tiat tpey had no written contrect. The samo pro-
portions vere found among persons renting land to others, Hovever, a
majority of all tonure groups felt that a written contract is necessary,
RESTRIC TED

and an even larger majority felt that the conditions of the contraot should
bo written in detail. Sixty-two percont of all tho farmers preferred payment
of rent in cash rather t an in kind. A majority of all temure groups folt
that tonants should not be allowed to sublet land vithout the consent of the
owmer.
Attitudes Toward the Local Land Commissions
A large majority of tne farmers stated that they had voted in the land
commission olections of 1946. Nearly tvo-thirds of them thought tint their
local commission mombers were the bost porsons for t e job.
The methed usod by the land commissions to determine individual eligi-
bility to purchase land was approved by a majority. Higher percentages of
ownor-cultivators, people who folt thet the land reform program had boen bene-
ficial to their family, and people cultivating larger plots of land, thnn of
the responlents as a whole, approved of the mothod used.
A majority stated without qualification that they thought that those
villagers who had bought land during the program were satisfied witn the
handling of their cases by the land commission and that they thomselves were
satisfied with the hendling of thoir om cases.
The principlo of price controls for t e sale of land, administered by.
the local land commissions, was favored by a majority, although one-fifth
of all farmers thought that t e matter was one to be settled either by the
commission or by the concerned partios, depending upon the circumstances.
When asked to select tho proper method of settling problems arising from
delay in paying rent on the part of a tenant, 78 percent of the farmers re-
plied t at tho land commission should settle the issue in some or all circum-
stances. Tenants tonded to favor settlement by the land commission and
landlords settloment by consultation between the concerned parties.
Attitudes Tovard the Village Agricultural Cooperatives
Tho village agricultural cooperatives aro visited often by two-thirds
of the farmers, although 13 percent of tho post-land reform lnndlords re-
ported that they never go to the cooperative. A majority of all tenure
groups expressed qualified confidonce in the prosent officials of the co-
operative and over one-quarter exprossed full confidence. About a third of
the farmers expressed a favorable attitude toward the inclusion of women
among the officinls, whilo 43 percent thought that it vould make no difference.
Attitudes Toward the Inheritance of Land
Over three-quarters of the farmers vhose familics had previously pos-
sessed land reported thet primogeniture has been the custom in their family
in the past, and only four percent reported that the land has been equally
divided among dil sons.

ATTITUDES OF FARIERS TOWARD RURAL PROBLEMS
Presented with a hypothetical situation in which they had two mons and
one daughtor, and could act as they pleased, only 16 percent of the farm
stated simply that they would give their land to ths eldost son. Almost
half of the farmors wished to give the land to the son who would begome a
farmer.
When asked if t ey thought that t e eldest son should be exoused from
his traditional duties to the family if the land were divided equally,
farmers of all temure groups expressed divided opinion: 40 percent said
"yes," 50 percent said "no," and 10 percent were undecided.
Two-thirds of the farmers thought that if all the sons and daughters
were given land equnlly the family would be unable to get along.
Use of Information Media
A large majority of the farmers reported that they take a newspaper.
Subscriptions are almost ovenly divided between a national pmper and a.local
or a rogional mper. Over 80 percent of the subscribers wore satisfied with
their present neuspaper, and only 12 percent wanted to change.
Radio ownership averages 64 percent, and eppears to have risen in the
past 18 months. Higher percentagos of owner-cultivators, landlords, and
cultivators of tho larger tracts of lend own radios ten do tenants and
smaller operators. A majority of all respondents reported that they listen
both to tho "Early Bird" and the "New Farm Villago" radio programs.

Chapter III
ATTITU DES TOARD THE LAND REFORM PROGRAM.
In the early days of the land reform, critics expressed some doubt
concerning the reaction of Japanese farmers. However, at the time of the
present survey, the greatest part of the farmcrs, especially of the former
tenants, was satisfied with the land reform program. One-half of all the
respondents, and over two-thirds of those who had been tenants prior to
the land reform program, reported that their families had been benefited.
More than one-haif of all the respondents and exactly two-thirds: of the
tenants felt that the land reform program had been beneficial to their
village. As expocted, the program was unpopular among the former landlords.
The majority of them reported that the program had not been beneficial to
their family, and only 37 percent stated that the program had been good for
their village.

ATTITUDES OF FARMERS TOWARD RURAL PROBLEMS
The correlations between pre-land raform-tenancy status and post- land
reform attitudes deserve special emphasis. Tenants, for whose betterment
the program was initiated, expressed the greatest enthusiasm. Only eight
percent of them thought that conditions in the village would have been better
had there been no land reform, while 47 percent thought they would be worse,
and 30 percent that they would not have been different. Landlords, on the
other hand, vere more critical. That numbers of them are able to be ob-
jective concerning general village conditions is attested to by the fact
that a large majority of them felt that the land reform program had been
either good or both good and bad for the village, while only 16 perdent of
them thought that it had definitely been bad. However, only eight percent
of the landlords felt that the program had been beneficial to their own
family, and a majority,of them, 6l percent, thought definitely that it had
not. The distinction that landlords seem to make between "good for. the
village" and "good for my family" is demonstrated by several facts. Only a
slightly higher percentage of landlords than of other groups thought that
living conditions in the village would have been better had there been no
land reform, and 21 percent of the landlords expressed the belief that con-
ditions would have been worse.
The land reform program did not seek to destory tenancy completely,
but rather to reduce it to a minimum and to eliminate its evils. Although
absentee landlordism was totally eliminated, resident landlords were per-
mitted to rotain a certain amount of land for tenant cultivation under con-
ditions specified by law. In April 1949, the total area thus retained and
cultivated by tenants was about 600,000 cho, or approximately 10 percent
of t e total arable land in Japan. Critics of the land reform program have..
contended that these retention provisions are inimical to the stated purpose
of the legislation, and are an invitation to the landlords to maintain their
traditional control in the rural community. Supporters of the law have re..
plied that the margin of permissible tenancy which these provisions allow.
is sound for farm management reasons, as it pormits that measure of flex-.
ibility required to adjust tenure conditions to tho changing social and
economio requiremonts of individual farm operators. In the present survey,
all respondents were asked.how they felt about this much discussed point.
Thirty-four percent of them thought that this retention should be permitted,
50 percent thought that it should not, and 16 percent stated that they did
not know. As might be expected, tenants were the most critical of the pro-
vision, although 20 percent of them favored tho law as it stands.

While the general level of satisfaction with the land reform program
seems to be high, many specific complaints concerning inadequacies of the
program, local conditions, and unfairness were voiced by farmers. Twenty
four percent of the farmers interviewed offered specific observations? The
most frequently mentioned points of dissatisfaction were the following:
landlords should not have been permitted to retain so much land oultivated
by others; land purchase was unduly complex; and forest, meadow, and un-
reclaimed land should have been included in tie program. Many of the points
relate to local conditions or individual cases, for example, claims of un-
fair treatment by the land commission. Others, however, such as the sug-
gestion that other lands be included within the categories of land subject
to reform, touch on broader problems. The release of forest land, for
example, is considered by some farmers to be a pressing problem in mountain-
ou areas.

RESTRICTED
Chapter IV
LANDLORD-TENANT RELATIONS
Before the land reform program was put into effect, the Japanese tenant
farmer was' substantially at the mercy of his landlord. Contractual arrange-
ments were rarely formal and explicit, and in cases of doubt the landlords
interpretations generally prevailed. A majority of these arrangements were
oral. Under traditional tenancy, the land rental was commonly paid in kind,
rather than in cash. This arrangement was frequently a burden upon tenants,
for the rent was usually a stipulated amount of grain, set by the landlords,
'and determined before the harvest on the basis of yields harvested during
good seasons. In the event of a bad harvest, a tenant had to turn over a
majority of his total crop to his landlord as rent, and buy food to eat at
the high prices common during a bad harvest year.
The provisions of land reform legislation stipulated changes in these
conditions. In order to provide tenants with security of tenure and pro-
tection from dishonest landlords, the legislation provided that all tenant-
landlord agreements be written, and that all written contracts be registered
at the village office. It was also stipulated that contracts would specify
the amount of farm rent, the terms of payment, the period of temure, the
conditions which would warrant rent reductions, and the provisions under which
certain expenses incurred by the tenant would be shared by the landlord and
under which remuneration for immovable improvements would be granted tenants
in case of lease termination.
At the time the survey was made, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry's
program for promoting tenancy agreements had been in effect only six months.
Nevertheless, eight percent of all farmers renting land from others reported
that they had a written tenancy contract for all of the land, and 22 percent
reported that they had a contract for part of their land, while 70 percent
reported that they had no written contract for the land they rented from
others. The same proportions were found among persons renting land to others.

Chapter V
ATTITU DES TOWAR THE LOCAL LAND COMMISSIONS
Land reform legislation provided for the election of a local land com-
mission in every city, town, and village in Japan to administer the program.
As the executive organs of the land reform program, the local land com-
missions drafted the purchase plan for each village, determined the suit-
ability of lands to be purchased, established prices within legal limits,
verified the eligibility of purchasers, decided cases requiring unusual or
special treatment, and effected the actual purchase and resale of land. Each
local commission is composed of five tenants, three landowners, and two
owner-cultivators, who were originally elected to serve for a two-year term.
Elections were held during December of 1946. In only 52 percent of the cases,
among the 10,777 local commissions, was it necessary to hold elections,
since, in the other cases, the number of candidates equalled the number of
positions to be filled.1 The lists of the electors were open to public in-
spection. Persons eligible to vote in the elections were those who were
at least 20 years of age and members of households cultivating one tan or
more of land in Japan proper and three tan in Hokkaido. It is assumed in
this analysis that many of the respondents who said that they had voted in
the elections actually meant that they participated in the selection of a
buraku nominee. 2
A large majority of the respondents stated that they had voted in the
land commission elections. Nearly two-thirds of them thought that their
local commission members were the best people for the job, although one-fifth
1. In the majority of those villages in which commissions were seated by
proclamation, nominees were selected by domocratic means in each of tho
buraku of the village. In accordanco with Japanese law, whenever the num-
ber of nominees equalled the number of positions open, no olections were
held, and the mayor simply soated the candidates by proclamation.
2. On the basis of the results of field investigations conducted from
6-15 January 1947, Occupation authorities beliove that from 75-80 percent
of the land commissions were accopted as satisfactory by their constituents,
and t at in the majority of cases in wlich no cloctions had boon held the
selection of candidatos had been accomplished according to democratic pro-
cedures. However, a few instances have been discovered in which there was
ovidenco of voting controlled by vosted community interosts. The Ministry
of Agriculture and Forestry took immediate steps to inform farmors of their
rights under tho law to recall commissioners who did not represent thon.
As a consoquonce, recall elections wore held in many villages.

Land reform legislation provides that the prico of land shall henoe
forth be dotermined by the land commissions, acting in accordance with prin
ciples established by law, rather than by negotiations between the conoerned
parties. This was one of tne issues which had caused the failure of all
previous land reform programs in Japan, for without price controls, a majority
of the tenants wero unable to purchase land. Furthermore, the prosent land
reform legislation stipulated that, in ordor to prevent speculation with
land purchased from the Govornmont, land could not be rosold privately, but
that all rosales would be made diroctly to tho Governmont on terms similar.
to those characterizing the original sale contract.
A majority of all farmers, and a majority of all tonure groups excopt
landlords, favored the principle of prico controls administerod by tho local
land commissions. However, one-fifth of all farmers and one-fourth of the
landlords and owner-cultivators, thought that the determination of price
should be made according to the circumstances. Nearly one-third of the land-
lords favorod negotiation.

Several provisions of land reform legislation protected the tenant by
guaranteeing him security of tenure. This factor had been tne cause of near-

tenure had been one of the greatest ovils of the tonancy system. Many
tenant cvictions were caused by the failure of tonants to pay the rent that
they had agreed to pay in tneir contracts. However, under a provision of
land reform legislation, all disputes arising out of any delay in paymont
of ront by the tenant for good cause must be settled by the land commission,
rather than by consultation betweon the concerned partios.

Chapter VI
ATTITUJES TOWARD THE VILLAGE AGRICULTURAL COOPERATIVES
Prior to the Occupation, monopoly agricultural associations, organized
in 1943, served as the instrumentality through which agricultural prices
and production were controlled, crop collections and rationing accomplished,
and rural capital utilized in accordance with national.policy. One local
agriucltural association (Nogyokai) was established in each city, town, and
village in Japan. These local associations were members of the national
association. The entire system was under the direct control of the Ministry
of Agriculture and Forestry. Membership in local associations was compulsory
for individual farmers.
Legislation authorizing the formation of agricultural cooperatives was
enacted by the Diet in 1947. The goals of the legislation were to release
farmers from the domination of non-agrarian interests and to afford them
the opportunity to organize independent cooperatives. Under the provisions
of the legislation, 15 or more farmers may organize a cooperative, any num-
ber of cooperatives may be formed in one village, farmers may belong to
more than one cooperative, and each member farmer is entitled to one vote in
his cooperative. Cooperatives may perform banking, marketing, purchasing,
mutual relief, and other services to farmers. In addition, the Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry has urged cooperatives to perform educational and
recreational functions, and has stressed the fact that if the cooperatives
are to be truly democratic the women of the community should be afforded full
opportunity to participate in the activities. Some of the new cooperatives
had been in operation for as long as five months at the time the survey was
made, and the agricultural associations were all dissolved by 14 August 1948.
Since their dissolution, the agricultural associations have been engaged
in terminating their affairs.
In order to determine the extent to which farmers have direct contact
with a cooperative, respondents were asked if they visited their cooperative
often. About two-thirds reported that they did, and only three percent
stated that they never visit the cooperative. Post-land reform landlords
reported the least frequent regular visits to the cooperative. Pre-land
reform tenants and owmer-cultivators, and post-land reform owner-cultivators,
reported the most frequent regular visits. Only a small percentage of these
groups, in contrast to 13 percent of the post-land reform landlords, reported
that they never visit the cooperative.

Chapter VII
ATTITUDES TOWARD T E INHERITANCE OF LAND
Traditionally, land has been inhorited in Japan chiefly by primogopi-
ture. Furthermore, the position of tho head of ti:e household was also
passed on by succession -- not always to tho same person who inherited the
land. These inhoritanco patterns wero mattors of practico, and had as
logal sanotions provisions in the Civil Code. Howevor, Japan's amondod
Civil Code provides that all children and rolatives shall havo the right
to sharo in t'ie inhoritanco, including land.I Tho pattem of succession
through heads of households was climinated completely in this now codo.
Many will agrec that these provisions aid in tie growth of tho individual,
and will permit young peoplo to develop a groator degreo of solf-direction,
but it is also widely recognizod thet in Japan thoy prosont a complicated
oconomic problom. Most farm holdings in Japan are too small to bo sub-
divided and continuo to provide subsistenco to a family, and it has only
been tho migration of younger sons into the cities and their conscription
into the arny that has enabled Japan's farm communitios to maintain some
semblance of belanco betwoon land rosources and population. The attitudos
and behavior of the individual farmer will ultimately decido the extent to
which these provisions of the Civil Codo are put into practice.
Respondonts wero first asked tho form of inheritanco practiced tra-
ditionally in their family. Over t ree-querters of tho heads of housoholds
whoso families had previously possessod land reported that primogeniture
had been the custom in their family in the past, and only four percent ro-
ported that tho land has boen oqually divided among all sons.

An important element of the Japanese family system is its emphasis
upon mutual obligations and responsibilities for the welfare of the mem-
bers. A more successful member of a family is obligated to give aid to
less successful members. This applies particularly to the oldest son, if
ho is the son who has inherited the land. Along with the land, he inherits
tho rosponsibility for the care of his paronts, and for other members of
his close family as well, if they should need aid. When asked if thoy
thought the eldest son should be excused from porforming these traditional
dutios if the land wore divided equally, rospondonts of all tenure groups
expressed divided opinion.
1. Students of Japanese rural society have known for some time that there
has been some deviation from the primogeniture pattern, in particular, that
a son other than the eldost is ofton solectod to inhorit the land. The
plurality of the "to tho son who will become a farmer" rosponse, therefore,
would seem to reflect an existing custom. It may bo spoculated that the
rolatively high porcentage of tho combinod "equally to all" rosponses might
have been caused either by a knowledge of the Civil Codo or by a general
senso of "justice" which the oconomic realitios or rural lifo mako im-
possible of fulfillmont.

The Department desires the following information from Natural
Resources Section SCAP in respect to amendments to Agricultural Land
Adjustment Law passed by Diet on May 23, 1949: Official interpre-
tation of Article 15-2 (as amended) dealing with membership on
land commissions including the catagories (1, 2, or 3) in which the
894.52/7-3049
following groups belong: Landowners (non-cultivating); landowner-
cultivators (cultivating part of their land and leasing out part);
landowner-cultivators (cultivating all their land); part-uwmers -
part-tenants (landowners who also rent land); full tenants
(cultivators owning no land). Will other amendments passed to ALAL
tend to increase land rentals and land tenancy?

Subject: Amendments to Japanese Agricultural Land Adjustment Law.
L
AGRI
The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to refer to the
Department's airgram instruction no. 126 of July 30, 1949, requesting
certain information on amendments to the Agricultural Land Adjustment
1/
Act as passed by the Diet on May 23, 1949. Enclosed is a copy of a
leaflet prepared by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry on the
occasion of the second General Election of Agricultural Land Commis-
sions, and quoted in full in (SCAP) Natural Resources Section Weekly
j
Summary no. 19l; the leaflet contains an official interpretation of
Article 15-2 of the recent amendments.
It will be noted that tenant farmers who lease and cultivate more
than 2 tan (5 tan in Hokkaido) of land fall in Category I (lessees),
while farmers who let out on lease more than 2 tan (again 5 tan in
Hokkaido) fall in Category II (lessors). All other farmers fall with-
in Category III. In the case of farmers who both lease and let out
on lease, the balance of the two figures determines status for pur-
poses of election to the Land Commissions; a farmer leasing 1 tan
and letting out on lease 3 tan, for instance, is deemed to fall with-
in Category II. Lessors or lessees of less than 2 tan, and non-
renting landowners, fall within Category III. (One tan equals 0.245064
acres.)
It is the opinion of the Natural Resources Section that the amend-
ments will not lead to any increase in farm tenancy. Limitations on
the transfer of ownership or lease rights to farm land remain substan-
tially as they were under the unamended law, and insure that persons
acquiring such rights intend to cultivate the land, and that land hold-
ings will not exceed the retention limits set by the Owner Farmer Estab-
1ishment Special Measures Law (Law no. 43 of 1946). Thus Article 4 of
the amended law, while retaining the provision that the permission of
the Agricultural Land Commission must be obtained for the transfer of
land rights, specifies instances in which permission may not be granted,
or may be granted only with the concurrence of the Minister of Agri-
culture and Forestry. Thus, the Natural Resources Section concludes,
there is no more likelihood of an increase of tenancy under the new
law than under the old.
The
UNCLASSIFIED
M
49

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 604,
September 1, 1949.
-2-
The recent amendments to t he Agricultural Land Adjustment Law
have no relation to the fixing of land rentals. The Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry, however, is studying the possibility of
an upward revision of rentals, within the framework of Article 9-8
of the Law, which limits rentals to 25 per cent of the value of a
normal yield in the case of paddy lands, and 15 per cent in the
case of dry fields.
The Natural Resources Section reports further that Mr. L. I.
HEWES, Jr., land reform expert in that section, will be available
for consultation in Washington from the end of September. . It is
considered possible that the Department may wish to obtain further.
clarification of the recent amendments from Mr. Hewes, who may be
reached at the Cosomos Club, Madison Place and H Street, NW,
Washington.

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 604,

Tokyo, on the subject "Amendments to

(COPY)
ITEM 1 NATURAL RESOURCES SECTION WEEKLY SUMMARY NO. 191
MINISTRY OF AGRICULTURE AND FORESTRY INFORMATION LEAFLET ON
SECOND-GENERAL ELECTION OF AGRICULTURAL LAND COMMISSIONERS
Hello! Everybody!
All Agricultural Land Commissioners in Japan soon wdll be chosen
again in a general election for the first time since your Agricultural
Land Commissions were set up.
The voting day is August 18, 1949
The method for the forthcoming election is much different from
the last one; you will understand the difference when you read this
leaflet carefully.
Persons having voting rights are all requested to go to the pol-
ling place, calling for one another on their way.
This is an important election for you farmers.
Let us elect your true representatives as commissioners of the
Agricultural Land Commission, who handle the matters relating to the
land cultivated by you.
A new voters' list of those qualified for voting will be pre-
pared for the forthcoming election.
Have you confirmed whether your names are correctly put down in
the voters' list?
Everybody can inspect during the 5-day period starting 1 August
the voters' list at t he public office (or other designated places).
The Election Control Commission officially announces the place
and date on which you can look into the list.
When you go to find whether your names are omitted or misspelled
go directly to the Agricultural Land Commission and ask it to certify
the matter; then apply to the Election Control Commission for the re-
vision. Take care about this, for the Commission will not accept your
application if you do not apply during the period of 5 days for in-
spection.
A household cultivating more than 1 tan (in Hokkaido, more than
3 tan) or a household owning agricultural land of more than 1 tan (in
Hokkaido, more than 3 tan) has members with voting rights; persons
more than 20 years old in such households are qualified to vote and
are eligible for election.
However
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 604,
September 1, 1949.
-2-
However, persons who live in the household but are not full time
farmers (this is certified by the Agricultural Land Commission) or owm
no agricultural land, are neither qualified to vote nor eligible for
election.
In election, you farmers are divided into 3 groups and these
groups conduct the election respectively.
Be careful about the divisions, for they are quite different from
the old divisions of "Tenant farmers", "Landowners" and "Owner-farmers".
The structure of the new Agricultural Land Commission is as fol-
lows:
Representatives of the lessees (Group No. 1):
2 persons ---- Elected from among those farmers who lease
and cultivate agricultural land of more than
2 tan (in Hokkaido, 5 tan).
Representatives of the lessors (Group No. 2):
2 persons ---- Elected from among those farmers who let out
on lease their own agricultural land of more
than 2 tan (in Hokkaido, 5 tan).
Representatives of the others (Group No. 3):
6 persons ---- Elected from among those farmers not included
in the foregoing groups.
Farmers will choose by vote, from among their respective groups,
the persons who will stand for the Agricultural Land Commission.
As for farmers who lease one lot of agricultural land and let out
on lease another lot, the group to which they belong is decided by the
difference in acreage of the two lots.
Which group do you belong to?
Just look at the following instances:
(a) (A person who is an owner-cultivator of 2 cho and a lessee of
3 tan.)
---- Group No. 1 (The acreage cultivated by the owner-cultivator
does not matter, for he leases more than 2 tan.)
(b) A person who is an owner-cultivator of 3 tan and lessor of 3 tan.
---- Group No. 2 (The acreage cultivated by the owner-culti-
vator does not matter, for he lets out on lease more than 2 tan.)
(c) A person who is a lessee of 4 tan and a lessor of 1 tan.
---- Group No. 1 (He leases a balance of 3 tan.)
(d) A person who is a lessee of 4 tan and a lessor of 3 tan.
---- Group No. 3 (He leases a balance of 1 tan.)
(e) A non-cultivator land owner of 2 tan and less
(f) A full-time tenant farmer of 2 tan and less
(g) An
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 341 === (confidence 1.00)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 604,
September 1, 1949.
-3-
(g) An owner-cultivator of 1 cho and at the same time lessee
of 1 tan.
----- Group No. 3 (They are not included in Group No. 1
leasing more than 2 tan, nor in Group No. 2 letting out
on lease more than 2 tan.)
What sort of person do you elect as Conmissioners of the New
Agricultural and Land Commission?
Let's elect persons willing to do their best to protect your
rights, and to promote agriculture in your village and freedom for
working farm households.
Is there a person among those who stand for the Agricultural
Land Commission, who does not grasp the spirit of land reform, who
is swayed only by his interest, friendship or favoritism, who intends
to make money or take a bribe because of his position as commissioner,
and who will remain idle, making no positive efforts worthy of your
representative, even if he does nothing wrong?
If you elect such a person by mistake, you will repent it after-
ward.
Election is not other people's concern. If the person you want
to be your representative on the Agricultural Land Commission is not
already a candidate, then ask him to be a candidate immediately; he
can give notice of his candidature within a week before voting day.
The business of the future Agricultural Land Commission will be
more and more important.
Up to now the Agricultural Land Commission has been occupied in
the routine work relating largely to purchase and sale of agricultural
land, but in future the Commission will be concerned with all the pro-
blems facing you and will seek to settle them so as to attain the end
of setting farmers free from old traditions;
For example,
(1) In order to carry out fairly the transfer of agricultural
land and pasture land, purchase and sale have to be carefully executed;
to assure that these transfers are properly retained, control is given
over purchase and sale of agricultural land, its destruction and the
transfer of the right to cultivate. To this end, we must carefully pre-
pare the correct ledger and map of cultivated land.
(2) In order to modernize management, the program for consolidating
agricultural lands through exchange, partition and annexation has been
mapped out, and will be pushed forward.
(3) Control is given over the transfer of forests producting fire-
wood and charcoal, and over grassland as well as grazing land so as to
protect farmers' utilization rights; furthermore, service will be pro-
vided in maintaining these rights.
(4) When
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 604,
September 1, 1949.
-4-
(4) When disputes break out centering around utilization of
agricultural land (e.g., tenancy disputes, boundary disputes, water
utilization disputes, and cutting off trees), the Agricultural Land
Commission intervenes between the disputing parties and seeks to
bring about an impartial settlement.
(5) The Commission seeks contracts that will provide equitable
farm rent and other tenancy conditions, and seeks to protect tenant-
rights through drawing written contracts and registering them.
Besides, the Commission will deal with other important problems
about land and also will assist you in getting information.
Do you know what is meant by the word, "Recall"?
When the Commissioners of your choice do not perform their duties
properly, you will prepare an application under joint signature by one
half or more of the voters of the group to which you belong, and then
submit it to the Election Control Commission in a city, town or vil-
lage; this will bring a mass resignation of the Commissioners of the
particular group and a new election within 2 weeks.
In this way you can keep constant watch over your representatives,
and dismiss them if they are unsuitable.
This procedure is called "Recall", but no recall can be exercised
until 6 months after the election.
(Marginal notes)
Is your name on the voters' list for election of the Commis-
sioners of the Agricultural Land Commission?
---- You cannot vote if your name is not on the voterst list.
---- Let's go right now to confirm it.
Who has stood for a Commissioner in your village?
---- A Commissioner is your public servant who does important
business for you.
- Let's choose an able person worthy of your confidence.
Do you know what business is done by the Agricultural Land
Commission?
- It protects your agricultural land and keeps farm villages,
where land already has been transferred, from returning to feudal
conditions.
Let's set up a good, strong Commission.
